\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}
in the definition is restricted to a swap for a single strategy:

\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i})=\begin{cases}s^{\prime\prime}_{i}&\text{if $s_{i}=s^{\prime}_{i}$}\\
s_{i}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

for some
s^{\prime}_{i},s^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}
.
However, the problem of computing this version of approximate SFCEs is rather easy.
It is because if we set
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
to the uniform distribution over
S
, then the error is always at most
1/(\min_{i\in N}|S_{i}|)
.
Hence, for
\epsilon\geq 1/(\min_{i\in N}|S_{i}|)
, computing it can be done easily.
On the other hand, for
\epsilon\leq 1/(\min_{i\in N}|S_{i}|)
, if we assume
|S_{i}|
has the same order for every
i\in N
, then we can straightforwardly run an internal regret minimization algorithm with decision space
S_{i}
in time polynomial in
1/\epsilon=\min_{i\in N}|S_{i}|
.

To compare SFCEs with other classes defined on
\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
, we define the set of distributions obtained by mapping SFCEs to
\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
by
\eta\colon\Delta(S)\to\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
(see
Section

2.1
for the definition of
\eta
).
Formally, it is defined as
\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}=\{\eta(\sigma)\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}\mid\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}\}
.
From the definition, every
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}
is strategy-representable.

As we will see,
\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}
is a subset of approximate communication equilibria.
Informally, computing an
\epsilon
-SFCE is harder than computing an
\epsilon
-approximate communication equilibrium.
If an
\epsilon
-SFCE
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
can be obtained, then we can compute its corresponding distribution
\eta(\sigma)\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
, which is also an
\epsilon
-approximate communication equilibrium.
Note that this reduction is informal because distributions in
\Delta(S)
and
\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
have an exponentially large support in general, and the computational complexity of computing
\eta(\sigma)
depends on the succinct representation of
\sigma
.

Whether
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
is an SFCE cannot be determined by its corresponding
\eta(\sigma)\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
.
As in the following example, even if
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Delta(S)
correspond to the same
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
, it is possible that
\sigma
is an SFCE, but
\sigma^{\prime}
is not.

Example 2.9
.

Suppose
\Theta_{1}=\{\theta_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{1}\}
and
\Theta_{2}=\{\theta_{2}\}
with
A_{1}=\{a_{1},a^{\prime}_{1}\}
and
A_{2}=\{a_{2},a^{\prime}_{2}\}
.
The prior distribution
\rho\in\Delta(\Theta)
is the uniform distribution over
\Theta
.
Let
v_{1}(\tilde{\theta};a_{1},a_{2})=v_{1}(\tilde{\theta};a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2})=1
and
v_{1}(\tilde{\theta};a^{\prime}_{1},a_{2})=v_{1}(\tilde{\theta};a_{1},a^{\prime}_{2})=0
for any
\tilde{\theta}\in\Theta
,
while the second player’s payoff is always
0
, i.e.,
v_{2}\equiv 0
.
We consider two distinct distributions
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Delta(S)
with the same corresponding
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
.
The first one
\sigma
is the uniform distribution over
S
.
The second one
\sigma^{\prime}
is the uniform distribution over
s^{1},s^{2},s^{3},s^{4}
defined as

\displaystyle s^{1}_{1}(\theta_{1})=a_{1},~s^{1}_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=a_{1},~s^{1}_{2}(\theta_{2})=a_{2},

\displaystyle s^{2}_{1}(\theta_{1})=a^{\prime}_{1},~s^{2}_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=a^{\prime}_{1},~s^{2}_{2}(\theta_{2})=a_{2},

\displaystyle s^{3}_{1}(\theta_{1})=a_{1},~s^{3}_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=a^{\prime}_{1},~s^{3}_{2}(\theta_{2})=a^{\prime}_{2},

\displaystyle s^{4}_{1}(\theta_{1})=a^{\prime}_{1},~s^{4}_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=a_{1},~s^{4}_{2}(\theta_{2})=a^{\prime}_{2}.

Both
\pi(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
and
\pi(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta_{2})
are the uniform distribution over
A_{1}\times A_{2}
.
The expected payoff for each type of
\theta_{1}
and
\theta^{\prime}_{1}
is
\frac{1}{2}
.

First, we show that
\sigma
is an SFCE.
Since
v_{2}\equiv 0
, the second player does not have any incentive to deviate.
Since
\sigma
is the uniform distribution over
S
, when
s_{1}\in S_{1}
is recommended, the posterior distribution of
s_{2}
is the uniform distribution over
S_{2}
.
Hence, the expected payoff of each action of
A_{1}
is
\frac{1}{2}
, and the first player does not have any incentive to deviate.

Next, we show that
\sigma^{\prime}
is not an SFCE.
Since
s^{1}_{1},s^{2}_{1},s^{3}_{1},s^{4}_{1}
are all distinct, the first player can infer the recommendation
s_{2}(\theta_{2})
for the second player.
The first player can increase the payoff to
1
by taking the same action as the second player.
Therefore,
\sigma^{\prime}
is not an SFCE.

2.4
Agent-normal-form correlated equilibria

Agent-normal-form correlated equilibria (ANFCEs) are defined as correlated equilibria of the agent normal form of a Bayesian game.
Recall that in the agent normal form, the same player with different types is hypothetically considered as distinct players.
In an ANFCE, the mediator recommends possibly correlated actions to all hypothetical players
(i,\theta_{i})\in N^{\prime}
.
Since an action profile for the agent normal form assigns some
a_{i}\in A_{i}
to every
i\in N
and
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, it can be identified with a strategy profile
s\in S
.
Hence, we can define ANFCEs as the set of distributions over
S
.
The only difference from SFCEs is the information that each player can use for deviations.
While each player
i\in N
can decide their action according to the recommended actions for all types
\Theta_{i}
in SFCEs, each player
i\in N
can use only the recommended action
s_{i}(\theta_{i})
for their realized type
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
in ANFCEs.
Here we define an approximate version of ANFCEs with additive error
\epsilon
.

Definition 2.10

(
\epsilon
-Approximate agent-normal-form correlated equilibria (
\epsilon
-ANFCEs))
.

For any
\epsilon\geq 0
, a distribution
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
is an
\epsilon
-approximate agent-normal-form correlated equilibrium
if
for any
i\in N
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i})),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\epsilon.

(
\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{ANF}}
)

Let
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Delta(S)
be the set of all
\epsilon
-ANFCEs.

In contrast to SFCEs, each player
i\in N
in ANFCEs is informed of only the action for the realized type
s_{i}(\theta_{i})
, not the full strategy
s_{i}
.
In an SFCE, each player can use recommendations for unrealized types, which differentiates SFCEs from ANFCEs.
In an ANFCE, each player
i\in N
with type
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
can use only
s_{i}(\theta_{i})
to decide deviation
\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i}))
.
This difference makes
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
broader than
\Sigma_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}
.

Remark 2.11
.

The incentive constraint in our definition is slightly stronger than the incentive constraint for each hypothetical player
(i,\theta^{\prime}_{i})\in N^{\prime}
, which can be written as

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta_{i}=\theta^{\prime}_{i}\}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta_{i}=\theta^{\prime}_{i}\}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}(s_{i}(\theta_{i})),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\epsilon

for any
\phi_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}\colon A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
This can be checked by letting
\phi(\theta^{\prime}_{i},\cdot)=\phi_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}
and
\phi(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\cdot)
be the identity map for each
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}\setminus\{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\}
in (

IC
ANF

).
Note that this difference is only for the error parameter, and these two incentive constraints are equivalent for
\epsilon=0
.

Since the setting of the agent normal form is hypothetical, it is difficult to consider a natural scenario for ANFCEs.
In an ANFCE, the mediator determines a strategy profile
s\in S
according to
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
but recommends to each player only
s_{i}(\theta_{i})
.
To realize it, the mediator must know the type profile
\theta\in\Theta
in advance.
However, if it is the case, then it is possible to realize any distribution
\pi(\theta)\in\Delta(A)
for each
\theta\in\Theta
, for which the notion of Bayesian solutions introduced later is more appropriate.

By extending no-swap-regret-dynamics to the agent normal form, we can obtain uncoupled dynamics converging to an ANFCE.
In these dynamics, for every time round
t\in[T]
, each hypothetical player
(i,\theta_{i})\in N^{\prime}
decides a distribution
\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i})\in\Delta(A_{i})
over their actions.
Hence, swap regret for each hypothetical player
(i,\theta^{\prime}_{i})\in N^{\prime}
considers an action swap
\phi_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}\colon A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
As with the incentive constraints discussed above, we consider the sum of swap regrets for all hypothetical players corresponding to
i\in N
, which we call
type-wise swap regret
.

Definition 2.12

(Type-wise swap regret)
.

For online learning with stochastic types specified by actions
A_{i}
, types
\Theta_{i}
, prior distribution
\rho_{i}
, and reward vector
u_{i}^{t}\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}
for each round
t\in[T]
, type-wise swap regret is defined as

R_{\mathsf{TS},i}^{T}=\max_{\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}))-u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})\right]\right].

For each
i\in N
, let
\mathcal{A}_{i}
be a subroutine that minimizes type-wise swap regret (ANFCEs) or untruthful swap regret (communication equilibria with strategy representability) for online learning with stochastic types;

for

each round
t=1,\dots,T

do

Each player
i\in N
decides their randomized strategy
\pi^{t}_{i}\in\Delta(A_{i})^{\Theta_{i}}
according to
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and shares it with the other players;

Each player
i\in N
computes reward
u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{-i}\sim\rho|\theta_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{-i}\sim\pi^{t}_{-i}(\theta_{-i})}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]
for every
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
, where
\pi^{t}_{-i}(\theta_{-i})\in\Delta(A_{-i})
is the product distribution that independently generates
a_{j}\sim\pi_{j}^{t}(\theta_{j})
for each
j\in N\setminus\{i\}
;

Feed
u_{i}^{t}\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}
to each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
as a reward vector for round
t
;

end for

Algorithm 2

Dynamics for ANFCEs or communication equilibria

As with SFCEs, by directly applying Theorem 3 of
Blum and Mansour 2007
to the agent normal form of Bayesian games, we obtain the following claim.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
\pi_{i}^{t}\in\Delta(A_{i})^{\Theta_{i}}
be the type-wise distribution of each player
i\in N
for each round
t\in[T]
in
Algorithm

2
.
Then, the empirical distribution
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
,
which is defined by
\sigma(s)=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\prod_{i\in N}\prod_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i};s_{i}(\theta_{i}))
for each
s\in S
,
is a
\frac{\max_{i\in N}R_{\mathsf{TS},i}^{T}}{T}
-ANFCE,
where
R_{\mathsf{TS},i}^{T}
is the type-wise swap regret for each subroutine
\mathcal{A}_{i}
.

Proof.

From the definition of
\epsilon
-ANFCEs, it is sufficient to prove

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i})),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;s(\theta))\right]\right]\leq\frac{\max_{j\in N}R_{\mathsf{TS},j}^{T}}{T}

for each
i\in N
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
Let
\sigma^{t}\in\Delta(S)
be the product distribution that independently generates
s_{j}(\theta_{j})\sim\pi_{j}^{t}(\theta_{j})
for each
j\in N
and
\theta_{j}\in\Theta_{j}
.
For each
\theta_{-i}\in\Theta_{-i}
, let
\pi_{-i}^{t}(\theta_{-i})\in\Delta(A_{-i})
be the product distribution that independently generates
a_{j}\sim\pi_{j}^{t}(\theta_{j})
for each
j\in N\setminus\{i\}
.
Then the left-hand side can be bounded as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i})),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;s(\theta))\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{t\sim[T]}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma^{t}}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i})),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))-v_{i}(\theta;s(\theta))\right]\right]\right]

(from the definition of
\sigma
)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{t\sim[T]}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i})}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{-i}\sim\pi_{-i}^{t}(\theta_{-i})}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})-v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\right]\right]

(from the definition of
\sigma^{t}
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i})}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{-i}\sim\rho|\theta_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{-i}\sim\pi_{-i}^{t}(\theta_{-i})}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})-v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}))-u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})\right]\right]

(from the definition of
u_{i}^{t}
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{R_{\mathsf{TS},i}^{T}}{T}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\max_{j\in N}R_{\mathsf{TS},j}^{T}}{T},

which completes the proof.
∎

A detailed description of the dynamics can be found in
Algorithm

2
.
We can obtain an algorithm for minimizing type-wise swap regret by running the swap regret minimization algorithm proposed by
Blum and Mansour 2007
separately for each
(i,\theta_{i})\in N^{\prime}
.
Since the rewards for each
(i,\theta_{i})\in N^{\prime}
are always bounded above by
\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})
, an upper bound of
O(\sqrt{T|A_{i}|\log|A_{i}|})
on swap regret directly applies to type-wise swap regret.

As with
\epsilon
-SFCEs, we can define its counterpart
\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
by
\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}=\eta(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon})
(see
Section

2.1
for the definition of
\eta
).
Since the incentive constraints for ANFCEs only rely on the action profile
s(\theta)
instead of the strategy profile
s
, we can characterize
\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
as follows.

Proposition 2.14
.

For any type-wise distribution
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
.

(ii)

\pi
is strategy-representable, and
for any
i\in N
and any
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]\right]-\epsilon.

(
\mathrm{IC}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{ANF}}
)

Proof.

First, we prove (ii) assuming (i).
Suppose
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
.
Since
\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}=\eta(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon})
, there exists
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
such that
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
.
From the definition of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
, the incentive constraint (

IC
ANF

) holds for every
i\in N
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
Since
\pi=\eta(\sigma)
, when
s
is generated from
\sigma
, the distribution of
s(\theta)
equals
\pi(\theta)
.
Then (

IC
ANF

) implies (

IC
′

ANF

).

Next, we prove (i) assuming (ii).
Suppose
\pi
is strategy-representable and satisfies the incentive constraint (

IC
′

ANF

) for every
i\in N
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
From the strategy representability, there exists
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
such that
\pi=\eta(\sigma)
.
As discussed above, when
s
is generated from
\sigma
, the distribution of
s(\theta)
equals
\pi(\theta)
.
Then (

IC
′

ANF

) implies (

IC
ANF

).
∎

2.5
Communication equilibria

A communication equilibria is defined as a type-wise distribution
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
that is incentive-compatible in the following scenario.
First, type profile
\theta\in\Theta
is jointly generated from prior distribution
\rho
, and then each player observes their own type
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
Next, the players privately report their own types to the mediator.
Then the mediator generates an action profile
a\in A
from
\pi(\theta)
and privately recommends
a_{i}
to each player
i\in N
.
If each player
i\in N
has no incentive to deviate from reporting the true type
\theta_{i}
and following the recommendation
a_{i}
, then this type-wise distribution
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
is called a communication equilibrium.

Formally, the incentive constraints for each player
i\in N
are defined for the following two mappings.
The first one
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
specifies a type
\psi(\theta_{i})
to be reported when the true type is
\theta_{i}
.
As a result, the distribution
\pi(\psi(\theta_{i}),\theta_{-i})
that generates the recommended action profile
a\in A
may be different from the original one
\pi(\theta)
.
The second one
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
specifies an action
\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i})
when
a_{i}
is recommended by the mediator and the true type is
\theta_{i}
.
If each player cannot gain by using any combination of
\psi
and
\phi
, a distribution
\pi
is a communication equilibrium.
Here we define its approximate version.

Definition 2.15

(
\epsilon
-Approximate communication equilibria)
.

For any
\epsilon\geq 0
, a type-wise distribution
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate communication equilibrium
if
for any
i\in N
,
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
, and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\psi(\theta_{i}),\theta_{-i})}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]\right]-\epsilon.

(
\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Com}}
)

Let
\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
be the set of all
\epsilon
-approximate communication equilibria.

A communication equilibrium can be defined as a Nash equilibrium for the extended game equipped with a
communication device
, which is a device that privately receives a signal from each player and then privately sends a stochastic (possibly correlated) signal to each player based on the received signals.
In general, the inputs and outputs for the communication device may be any signals different from type profiles
\Theta
and action profiles
A
.
By the revelation principle, it is sufficient to consider
direct
(or
canonical
) ones, in which the sets of inputs and outputs coincide with
\Theta
and
A

(
Myerson 1982
)
.
In this paper, we consider direct communication equilibria only.

There are two differences between communication equilibria and ANFCEs.
First, in an ANFCE, the mediator must generate a strategy profile
s\in S
before observing the type profile
\theta\in\Theta
, while in a communication equilibrium, the mediator can generate an action profile
a\in A
after observing the type profile.
Since there exists a type-wise distribution
\pi
that cannot be expressed by any strategy distribution (
Section

2.1
), ANFCEs are more restrictive than communication equilibria in this aspect.
Second, in an ANFCE, the players must inform the mediator of their true types, while in a communication equilibrium, the players can untruthfully report their types.
If the mediator can validate the reported types in some way, ANFCEs are more natural, but communication equilibria are more natural otherwise.

To introduce dynamics converging to a communication equilibrium, we present the definition of untruthful swap regret.
In addition to
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
that represents the deviation from the recommended action, the competitor can use
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
that represents untruthful type reporting.

Definition 2.16

(Untruthful swap regret)
.

For online learning with stochastic types specified by actions
A_{i}
, types
\Theta_{i}
, prior distribution
\rho_{i}
, and reward vector
u_{i}^{t}\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}
for every round
t\in[T]
, untruthful swap regret is defined as

R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}=\max_{\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}}\max_{\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi(\theta_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}))\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})\right]\right].

If we restrict
\psi
to the identity map, untruthful swap regret is reduced to type-wise swap regret.
Then untruthful swap regret is no less than type-wise swap regret.

Now we consider dynamics in which each player
i\in N
minimizes untruthful swap regret
R_{\mathsf{US},i}
.
In the following theorem, we show that these dynamics converge to a communication equilibrium.
Moreover, the empirical distribution is always strategy-representable from
Proposition

2.4
.

Theorem 2.17
.

Let
\pi_{i}^{t}\in\Delta(A_{i})^{\Theta_{i}}
be the type-wise distribution of each player
i\in N
for each round
t\in[T]
in
Algorithm

2
.
Then, the empirical distribution
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
,
which is defined by
\pi(\theta;a)=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\prod_{i\in N}\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i};a_{i})
for each
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
,
is a
\frac{\max_{i\in N}R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}}{T}
-approximate communication equilibrium that satisfies strategy representability,
where
R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}
is the untruthful swap regret for each subroutine
\mathcal{A}_{i}
.

Proof.

From
Proposition

2.4
,
\pi
is strategy-representable.
From the definition of
\epsilon
-approximate communication equilibria, it is sufficient to prove

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\psi(\theta_{i}),\theta_{-i})}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\leq\frac{\max_{j\in N}R_{\mathsf{US},j}^{T}}{T}

for each
i\in N
,
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
, and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
For each
t\in[T]
and
\theta\in\Theta
, let
\pi^{t}(\theta)\in\Delta(A)
be the product distribution that independently generates
a_{j}\sim\pi_{j}^{t}(\theta_{j})
for each
j\in N
.
Similarly, let
\pi_{-i}^{t}(\theta)\in\Delta(A_{-i})
be the product distribution that independently generates
a_{j}\sim\pi_{j}^{t}(\theta_{j})
for each
j\in N\setminus\{i\}
.
The left-hand side can be bounded as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\psi(\theta_{i}),\theta_{-i})}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{t\sim[T]}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi^{t}(\psi(\theta_{i}),\theta_{-i})}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi^{t}(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{-i}\sim\rho|\theta_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{-i}\sim\pi_{-i}^{t}(\theta_{-i})}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi(\theta_{i}))}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i})}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\right]\right]

(since
\pi^{t}(\theta)
is a product distribution)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi(\theta_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}))\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})\right]\right]

(from the definition of
u_{i}^{t}
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}}{T}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\max_{j\in N}R_{\mathsf{US},j}^{T}}{T},

which completes the proof.
∎

We present characterizations of communication equilibria with strategy representability, to which the proposed dynamics converge.

Proposition 2.18
.

For any type-wise distribution
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}
and
\pi
is strategy-representable.

(ii)

\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
.

(iii)

There exists some
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
such that
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
and

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\psi(\theta_{i}))),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\epsilon.

(
\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{ComSR}}
)

holds for any
i\in N
,
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
, and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.

Proof.

First, we prove (ii) assuming (i).
Suppose
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}
and
\pi
is strategy-representable.
Since (

IC
′

ANF

) is a weaker condition than (

IC
Com

),
\pi
satisfies (

IC
′

ANF

) for any
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
We thus obtain
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
from
Proposition

2.14
.

Next, we prove (iii) assuming (ii).
Suppose
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
.
Since
\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}=\eta(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon})
from the definition,
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
implies that there exists
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
such that
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
.
Moreover, since
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}
, (

IC
Com

) holds for any
i\in N
,
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
, and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
Since
\eta
is defined by
\pi(\theta;a)=\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s(\theta)=a)
for each
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
, the left-hand side of (

IC
Com

) is equal to the left-hand side of (

IC
ComSR

).
Then it is sufficient to show the equality of their right-hand sides.
Again from the definition of
\eta
, for any
\theta\in\Theta
, we have

\displaystyle\pi\left(\psi(\theta_{i}),\theta_{-i};a\right)=\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}\left(s_{i}(\psi(\theta_{i}))=a_{i},~s_{-i}(\theta_{-i})=a_{-i}\right).

Hence, for each fixed
\theta\in\Theta
, the distribution of
a
in the right-hand side of (

IC
Com

) equals the distribution of
(s_{i}(\psi(\theta_{i})),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))
in the right-hand side of (

IC
ComSR

), and then the right-hand sides are equal.

Finally, we prove (i) assuming (iii).
The existence of
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
such that
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
implies the strategy representability of
\pi
.
It is sufficient to prove that (

IC
Com

) holds for any
i\in N
,
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
, and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
Since (

IC
ComSR

) and (

IC
Com

) are equivalent as proved above, we obtain
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}
.
∎

2.6
Relations among classes of Bayes correlated equilibria

Here we compare the classes of Bayes correlated equilibria and show their relations.
First, we define an approximate version of
Bayesian solutions
(also called
partial Bayesian approach
)
(
Forges 1993
)
.

Definition 2.19

(
\epsilon
-approximate Bayesian solutions)
.

For any
\epsilon\geq 0
, a type-wise distribution
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate Bayesian solution
if
for any
i\in N
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]\right]-\epsilon.

(
\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{BS}}
)

Let
\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
be the set of all
\epsilon
-approximate Bayesian solutions.

Forges 1993
showed the relations among the classes in terms of the set of players’ payoff values.
Formally, if we denote the expected payoff vector achieved by
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
by

v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\pi)=\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\right)_{i\in N}\in[0,1]^{N},

then
Forges 1993
showed
v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{0})\subseteq v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0})\cap v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})
and
v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0})\cup v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})\subseteq v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{0})
.
Moreover,
Forges 1993
showed that these inclusions are strict, and
v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0})\not\subseteq v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})
and
v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})\not\subseteq v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0})
for some Bayesian games.

Here we show the same inclusion relations among the approximate versions in terms of type-wise distributions, that is,

\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}\quad\text{and}\quad\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}\cup\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}

for each
\epsilon\geq 0
.
We also show that these inclusion relations are strict.
First, we show the following relation.

Proposition 2.20
.

For any Bayesian game, it holds that
\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}
for any
\epsilon\geq 0
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}
be an arbitrary
\epsilon
-SFCE and
\pi=\eta(\sigma)\in\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}
its corresponding type-wise distribution.
From
Proposition

2.18
, it is sufficient to prove the incentive constraint (

IC
ComSR

) for each
i\in N
,
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
, and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
Since
\sigma
is an
\epsilon
-SFCE, the incentive constraint (

IC
SF

) holds for any
\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}
.
If we set
(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i})=\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\psi(\theta_{i})))
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
,
then it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\psi(\theta_{i}))),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\epsilon,

which completes the proof.
∎

Next, we provide an example of a Bayesian game in which
\pi\in(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})\setminus\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{0}
exists.
While deviations for SFCEs allow players to use recommendations for all other types, the deviations for communication equilibria allow players to use recommendations for at most one other type and ANFCE no other type.
We design a distribution such that a player can gain by using recommendations for two other types.
Then this distribution is a communication equilibrium and ANFCE but not SFCE.

Proposition 2.21
.

For some Bayesian game,
(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})\setminus\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{0}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

The distribution is defined as follows.
Let
\Theta_{1}=\{\theta_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1}\}
and
\Theta_{2}=\{\theta_{2}\}
with the uniform prior distribution
\rho
over
\Theta_{1}\times\Theta_{2}
.
Let
A_{1}=\{0,1,2,3,4\}
and
A_{2}=\{1,2,3,4\}
.
We define the first player’s payoffs for
\theta_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1}
by

\displaystyle v_{1}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2};a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}&\text{if $a_{1}=0$}\\
1&\text{if $a_{1}\neq 0$ and $a_{1}=a_{2}$}\\
0&\text{if $a_{1}\neq 0$ and $a_{1}\neq a_{2}$}.\end{cases}

\displaystyle v_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta_{2};a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}&\text{if $(a_{1},a_{2})\in\{(4,1),(4,2),(1,3),(1,4)\}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle v_{1}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1},\theta_{2};a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}&\text{if $(a_{1},a_{2})\in\{(2,1),(2,3),(3,2),(3,4)\}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The second player’s payoff
v_{2}
is defined as
v_{2}(\tilde{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2};a_{1},a_{2})=\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{1}\neq a_{2}\}}
for any
\tilde{\theta}_{1}\in\Theta_{1}
.
We consider
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
such that
\pi(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
,
\pi(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta_{2})
, and
\pi(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1},\theta_{2})
are the uniform distributions over
\{0\}\times\{1,2,3,4\}
,
\{(4,1),(4,2),(1,3),(1,4)\}
, and
\{(2,1),(2,3),(3,2),(3,4)\}
, respectively.

•

First, we show that
\pi
is strategy-representable by providing
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
such that
\pi=\eta(\sigma)
.
From the definition of
\pi(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
, it must hold that
s_{1}(\theta_{1})=0
with probability
1
.
From the definition of
\pi(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta_{2})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=4\mid s_{2}(\theta_{2})=1)=1

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=4\mid s_{2}(\theta_{2})=2)=1

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=1\mid s_{2}(\theta_{2})=3)=1

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=1\mid s_{2}(\theta_{2})=4)=1.

Similarly, from the definition of
\pi(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1},\theta_{2})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1})=2\mid s_{2}(\theta_{2})=1)=1

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1})=3\mid s_{2}(\theta_{2})=2)=1

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1})=2\mid s_{2}(\theta_{2})=3)=1

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1})=3\mid s_{2}(\theta_{2})=4)=1.

Hence,
\sigma
can be defined as

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta_{1})=0,~s_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=4,~s_{1}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1})=2,~s_{2}(\theta_{2})=1)=\frac{1}{4}

(4)

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta_{1})=0,~s_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=4,~s_{1}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1})=3,~s_{2}(\theta_{2})=2)=\frac{1}{4}

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta_{1})=0,~s_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=1,~s_{1}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1})=2,~s_{2}(\theta_{2})=3)=\frac{1}{4}

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s_{1}(\theta_{1})=0,~s_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=1,~s_{1}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1})=3,~s_{2}(\theta_{2})=4)=\frac{1}{4}.

Therefore,
\pi
is strategy-representable.

•

Next, we show that for any
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
, distribution
\pi
satisfies the incentive constraint for communication equilibria (

IC
Com

).
Since the second player’s payoff is at most
1
and the expected payoff is
1
under
\pi
, this player does not have any incentive to deviate from the recommendation.
Under distribution
\pi
, the first player always obtains payoff
\frac{1}{2}
, and then the expected payoff is
\frac{1}{2}
.
Then we show that the first player cannot gain by using any combination of
\psi
and
\phi
.
When the type is
\theta^{\prime}_{1}
or
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1}
, the payoff is at most
\frac{1}{2}
and the expected payoff is
\frac{1}{2}
under
\pi
, the first player does not have any incentive to deviate from the recommendation.
When the type is
\theta_{1}
, we consider the case of
\psi(\theta_{1})=\theta_{1}
and the others separately.
If
\psi(\theta_{1})=\theta_{1}
, then the first player is always recommended taking the action
0
, and the posterior distribution on the second player’s action is still the uniform distribution.
If
\psi(\theta_{1})=\theta^{\prime}_{1}
(or
\psi(\theta_{1})=\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1}
), then the first player can learn whether
s_{2}(\theta_{2})\in\{1,2\}
or
s_{2}(\theta_{2})\in\{3,4\}
(
s_{2}(\theta_{2})\in\{1,3\}
or
s_{2}(\theta_{2})\in\{2,4\}
, respectively).
By using this information, the first player can take the same action
s_{2}(\theta_{2})
with probability
1/2
, but the expected payoff is
\frac{1}{2}\cdot 1=\frac{1}{2}
, which is not better than the recommendation.
Therefore, the right-hand side is at most
\frac{1}{2}
for any
\psi
and
\phi
.

These two facts imply
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0}
from
Proposition

2.18
.
Finally, we show
\pi\not\in\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{0}
.
As discussed above, it is sufficient to consider
\sigma
that satisfies the conditions (
4
).
When the first player’s type is
\theta_{1}
, the first player can learn
s_{2}(\theta_{2})
from
s_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})
and
s_{1}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1})
.
Then
\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}
can be defined such that
(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{1}))(\theta_{1})=s_{2}(\theta_{2})
always holds.
This deviation provides the expected payoff
\frac{1}{3}\cdot 1+\frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac{1}{2}=\frac{2}{3}
to the first player, which is better than
\frac{1}{2}
obtained by the recommendation
\sigma
.
Therefore,
\sigma
is not an SFCE and
\pi\not\in\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{0}
.
∎

Remark 2.22
.

The example used in the proof also proves that
v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})\setminus v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{0})\neq\emptyset
for some Bayesian game.
This is stronger than the claim that
(v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0})\cap v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0}))\setminus v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{0})\neq\emptyset
for some Bayesian game, which was proved by
Forges 1993
with distinct
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0}
and
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0}
such that
v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\pi)=v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\pi^{\prime})
.

We can check
v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\pi)=(\frac{1}{2},1)\not\in v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{0})
as follows.
If the second player’s expected payoff is
1
, then
a_{1}\neq a_{2}
holds with probability
1
.
Hence, the first player with type
\theta_{1}
never obtains the payoff
1
.
To achieve the expected payoff
\frac{1}{2}
, the first player must achieve the payoff
\frac{1}{2}
with probability
1
, but then
\sigma
must satisfy the conditions (
4
).
As proved above,
\sigma
is not an SFCE.

Bayesian solutions

Next, we show that
\epsilon
-approximate Bayesian solutions contain the union of
\epsilon
-ANFCEs and
\epsilon
-approximate communication equilibria.

Proposition 2.23
.

For any Bayesian game, it holds that
\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}\cup\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}
for any
\epsilon\geq 0
.

Proof.

Assume
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
or
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}
.
If
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
, then
\pi
satisfies the incentive constraint (

IC
′

ANF

) for any
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
Since (

IC
BS

) is identical to (

IC
′

ANF

), this implies
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}
.
If
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}
, then
\pi
satisfies the incentive constraint (

IC
Com

) for any
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
By considering
\psi
as the identity map, we can obtain (

IC
BS

), which implies
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}
.
∎

This inclusion for
\epsilon=0
is strict for some Bayesian game.

Forges 1993
provided an example of a Bayesian game for which
v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{0})\setminus(v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0})\cup v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0}))\neq\emptyset
holds.
Since
v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0})\cup v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})=v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0}\cup\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})
holds in general,
v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{0})\setminus v_{\mathrm{EP}}(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0}\cup\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})\neq\emptyset
in this game, which implies
\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{0}\setminus(\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{0}\cup\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{0})\neq\emptyset
.

Bayes Nash equilibria

Finally, we consider the relations between Bayes Nash equilibria and the classes of Bayes correlated equilibria.
Recall that there are two interpretations of a Bayesian game: the strategic form and the agent normal form.
For each of these two different normal-form games corresponding to the same Bayesian game, we can consider the concept of Nash equilibria.
A well-known interesting fact is that these two concepts are equivalent.
This equilibrium concept is called
Bayes Nash equilibrium
.
Here we define approximate versions of Bayes Nash equilibria in the strategic form and the agent normal form, respectively.

First, we define Bayes Nash equilibria as Nash equilibria of the strategic form.
In the strategic form, each player
i\in N
independently decides a strategy
s_{i}\in S_{i}
according to some distribution
\sigma_{i}\in\Delta(S_{i})
.
Here we denote this Nash equilibrium by the product distribution of
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}
.
Let
\Sigma_{\mathsf{Prod}}\subseteq\Delta(S)
be the set of all product distributions, that is, for each
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{Prod}}
, there exists some
\sigma_{i}\in\Delta(S_{i})
for each
i\in N
such that
\sigma(s)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}(s_{i})
holds for every
s\in S
.
We then define an approximate version of Bayes Nash equilibria in the strategic form as follows.

Definition 2.24

(
\epsilon
-Approximate Bayes Nash equilibria (
\epsilon
-BNEs) in the strategic form)
.

For any
\epsilon\geq 0
, a distribution
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{Prod}}\subseteq\Delta(S)
is an
\epsilon
-approximate Bayes Nash equilibrium in the strategic form
if
for any
i\in N
and any
\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i}),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\epsilon.

(
\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{BNE}}
)

Let
\Sigma_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Delta(S)
be the set of all
\epsilon
-approximate Bayes Nash equilibria in the strategic form.

Next, we define Bayes Nash equilibria as Nash equilibria in the agent normal form.
In the agent normal form, each hypothetical player
(i,\theta_{i})\in N^{\prime}
decides a distribution
\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}\in\Delta(A_{i})
.
A type profile
\theta\in\Theta
sampled from
\rho\in\Delta(\Theta)
determines the active players,
whose actions
a_{i}\in A_{i}
are independently sampled from
\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}
for each
i\in N
.
Therefore, the action profile of the active players follows a type-wise product distribution
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}
.
Recall that we call
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
a type-wise product distribution if there exists some
\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}\in\Delta(A_{i})
for each
i\in N
and
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
such that
\pi(\theta;a)=\prod_{i\in N}\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}(a_{i})
for every
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
.
We define an approximate version of Bayes Nash equilibria in the agent normal form as follows.

Definition 2.25

(
\epsilon
-Approximate Bayes Nash equilibria (
\epsilon
-BNEs) in the agent normal form)
.

For any
\epsilon\geq 0
, a type-wise product distribution
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}\subseteq\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate Bayes Nash equilibria in the agent normal form
if
for any
i\in N
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]\right]-\epsilon.

(
\mathrm{IC}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{BNE}}
)

Let
\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
be the set of all
\epsilon
-approximate Bayes Nash equilibria in the agent normal form.

Here we show the equivalence of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
.
This is an approximate version of the well-known fact that Nash equilibria in the strategic form and the agent normal form are equivalent
(
Harsanyi 1967
)
, but we provide the proof for completeness.

Proposition 2.26
.

For any distribution
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
, its corresponding type-wise distribution satisfies
\eta(\sigma)\in\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
.
Conversely,
for any type-wise distribution
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
, there exists a distribution
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
such that
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
be any
\epsilon
-BNE in the strategic form and
\pi=\eta(\sigma)
.
First, we show that
\pi
is a type-wise product distribution.
Since
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{Prod}}
, there exists
\sigma_{i}\in\Delta(S_{i})
for each
i\in N
such that
\sigma(s)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}(s_{i})
holds for every
s\in S
.
For each
i\in N
and
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, define
\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}\in\Delta(A_{i})
by
\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}(a_{i})=\Pr_{s_{i}\sim\sigma_{i}}(s_{i}(\theta_{i})=a_{i})
for each
a_{i}\in A_{i}
.
Then

\pi(\theta;a)=\eta(\sigma)(\theta;a)=\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s(\theta)=a)=\prod_{i\in N}\Pr_{s_{i}\sim\sigma_{i}}(s_{i}(\theta_{i})=a_{i})=\prod_{i\in N}\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}(a_{i})

for every
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
,
which implies
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}
.

Next, we show the incentive constraint (

IC
′

BNE

) for each
i\in N
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
We use the incentive constraint (

IC
BNE

) for
\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}
defined by
(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i})=\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i}))
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
Since
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
implies that
s(\theta)
conforms to
\pi(\theta)\in\Delta(A)
, the left-hand side of (

IC
BNE

) equals the left-hand side of (

IC
′

BNE

).
The right-hand side of (

IC
BNE

) equals

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i}),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\epsilon

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i})),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\epsilon

(since
(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i})=\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i}))
)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]\right]-\epsilon,

(since
s(\theta)
conforms to
\pi(\theta)
)

which coincides with the right-hand side of (

IC
′

BNE

).
Therefore,
\pi
is an
\epsilon
-BNE in the agent normal form.

Conversely, let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
be any
\epsilon
-BNE in the agent normal form.
Since
\pi
is a type-wise product distribution, there exists
\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}\in\Delta(A_{i})
for each
i\in N
and
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
such that
\pi(\theta;a)=\prod_{i\in N}\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}(a_{i})
for every
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
.
We can define the corresponding
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
by
\sigma(s)=\prod_{i\in N}\prod_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}(s_{i}(\theta_{i}))
for every
s\in S
.
Then

\eta(\sigma)(\theta;a)=\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s(\theta)=a)=\prod_{i\in N}\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}(a_{i})=\pi(\theta;a)

for every
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
,
which implies
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
.

Next, we show
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
by proving
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{Prod}}
and the incentive constraint (

IC
BNE

) holds for any
i\in N
and
\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}
.
We can see
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{Prod}}
by expressing
\sigma
as
\sigma(s)=\prod_{i\in N}\sigma_{i}(s_{i})
for every
s\in S
where
\sigma_{i}\in\Delta(S_{i})
is defined by
\sigma_{i}(s_{i})=\prod_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\pi_{i,\theta_{i}}(s_{i}(\theta_{i}))
for every
s_{i}\in S_{i}
.
We then prove that (

IC
BNE

) holds for any
i\in N
and
\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}
by using (

IC
′

BNE

) for
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
defined by

\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i})\in\argmax_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{-i}\sim\rho|\theta_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{-i}\sim\sigma_{-i}}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a^{\prime}_{i},s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]

for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
,
where
\sigma_{-i}\in\Delta(S_{-i})
is the product distribution that independently generates
s_{j}\sim\sigma_{j}
for each
j\in N\setminus\{i\}
.
Note that
\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)
can be a constant function.
Since
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
, the left-hand side of (

IC
′

BNE

) equals the left-hand side of (

IC
BNE

).
The right-hand side of (

IC
′

BNE

) is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]\right]-\epsilon

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{-i}\sim\sigma_{-i}}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i})),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\epsilon

(since
s(\theta)
conforms to
\pi(\theta)
)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{-i}\sim\rho|\theta_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{-i}\sim\sigma_{-i}}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i})),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]\right]-\epsilon

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{-i}\sim\rho|\theta_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{-i}\sim\sigma_{-i}}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a^{\prime}_{i},s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]\right]-\epsilon

(from the definition of
\phi
)

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{i}\sim\sigma_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{-i}\sim\rho|\theta_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{-i}\sim\sigma_{-i}}\left[v_{i}(\theta;(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i}),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]\right]\right]-\epsilon

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i}),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]-\epsilon,

which implies (

IC
BNE

) for any
\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}
.
∎

Finally, we relate the classes of Bayes correlated equilibria to Bayes Nash equilibria.
The following proposition shows that if a type-wise product distribution is contained in each class of Bayes correlated equilibria, it is a Bayes Nash equilibrium.

Proposition 2.27
.

\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}=\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}=\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}=\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}=\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

From
Propositions

2.20
and
2.23
,
\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}
is a subset of
\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
,
\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}
, and
\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}
.
Moreover,
\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}
is a superset of
\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}
,
\Pi_{\mathsf{ANF}}^{\epsilon}
, and
\Pi_{\mathsf{Com}}^{\epsilon}
.
Therefore, it is sufficient to show that
\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
.

First, we prove
\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}
.
Suppose
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
.
From
Proposition

2.26
, there exists
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
such that
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
.
Since this
\sigma
satisfies (

IC
BNE

) for each
i\in N
and
\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}
, which is equivalent to (

IC
SF

).
Hence,
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}
, and then
\pi=\eta(\sigma)\in\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}
.
Moreover, from the definition of
\epsilon
-BNEs in the agent normal form,
\pi
is a type-wise product distribution, which implies
\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathsf{SF}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}
.

Next, we prove
\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}\subseteq\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
.
Suppose
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}\cap\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}
.
Since
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{BS}}^{\epsilon}
, the incentive constraint (

IC
BS

) holds for each
i\in N
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}
, which is equivalent to (

IC
′

BNE

).
Moreover,
\pi
is a type-wise product distribution since
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{Prod}}
.
From the definition of
\epsilon
-BNEs in the agent normal form, we obtain
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{BNE}}^{\epsilon}
.
∎

3
Algorithm for minimizing untruthful swap regret

In this section, we describe an efficient algorithm for minimizing untruthful swap regret, which leads to efficient computation of approximate communication equilibria.
In this section, we focus on the problem that a player
i\in N
is faced with, and therefore, the subscript
i
for
A_{i}
,
a_{i}
,
\Theta_{i}
,
\theta_{i}
,
\pi_{i}^{t}
, and
R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}
is not essential in this section.
We put the subscript
i
just for notational consistency.

We derive the algorithm in three steps:
First, we reduce untruthful swap regret minimization to a
\Phi
-regret minimization problem.
Then we apply the
\Phi
-regret minimization framework developed by
Gordon et al. 2008
and reduce it to an online linear optimization problem.
Finally, to obtain a regret upper bound optimal up to a multiplicative constant, we further decompose it to small external regret minimization problems.
The final algorithm is described in
Algorithm

3
.

Input:

The set of types
\Theta_{i}
and the set of actions
A_{i}
are specified in advance. The reward vector
u_{i}^{t}\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}
is given at the end of each round
t\in[T]
.

Initialize subroutines as follows:

•

let
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i}}
be a multiplicative weights algorithm with decision space
\Theta_{i}
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
;

•

let
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}
be a multiplicative weights algorithm with the doubling trick
(
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 1997
, Section 4.6)
with decision space
A_{i}
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
;

for

each round
t=1,\dots,T

do

Let
w^{t}_{\theta_{i}}\in\Delta(\Theta_{i})
be the output of
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i}}
in round
t
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
;

Let
y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}\in\Delta(A_{i})
be the output of
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}
in round
t
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
;

Define
Q^{t}\in[0,1]^{(\Theta_{i}\times A_{i})\times(\Theta_{i}\times A_{i})}
by
Q^{t}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))=w^{t}_{\theta_{i}}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}(a_{i})
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
;

Compute an eigenvector
x^{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}
of
Q^{t}
such that
Q^{t}x^{t}=x^{t}
and
(x^{t})^{\top}\mathbf{1}=|\Theta_{i}|
;

Decide the output
\pi_{i}^{t}\in\Delta(A_{i})^{\Theta_{i}}
by
\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i};a_{i})=x^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
;

Observe reward vector
u_{i}^{t}\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}
and feed reward vectors to subroutines as follows:

•

feed
\displaystyle\sum_{a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}(a_{i})\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i};a^{\prime}_{i})\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})
as the reward for decision
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}

to subroutine
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i}}
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
;

•

feed
\displaystyle\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i};a^{\prime}_{i})\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})
as the reward for decision
a_{i}\in A_{i}
to subroutine
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}

for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
;

end for

Algorithm 3

Algorithm for minimizing untruthful swap regret

3.1
Reduction to
\Phi
-regret minimization

First, we interpret untruthful swap regret minimization as
\Phi
-regret minimization with decision space
\mathcal{X}\subseteq[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}
instead of
\Delta(A_{i})^{\Theta_{i}}
, where

\mathcal{X}=\left\{x\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}\;\middle|\;\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}x(\theta_{i},a_{i})=1~(\forall\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i})\right\}

(5)

is the set of vectors corresponding to
\Delta(A_{i})^{\Theta_{i}}
.
Each
\pi_{i}\in\Delta(A_{i})^{\Theta_{i}}
is associated to some
x\in\mathcal{X}
by
x(\theta_{i},a_{i})=\pi_{i}(\theta_{i};a_{i})
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
.
Let
x^{t}\in\mathcal{X}
be the output of the algorithm in each round
t\in[T]
that corresponds to
\pi_{i}^{t}
.
Let
\bar{u}^{t}\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}
be the reward vector weighted by prior probabilities
\rho_{i}
, i.e.,
\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})=\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
.

Now we prove that the competitor is supposed to choose an optimal transformation of all
(x^{t})_{t=1}^{T}
from the set of linear transformations defined by

\mathcal{Q}=\left\{Q\in[0,1]^{(\Theta_{i}\times A_{i})\times(\Theta_{i}\times A_{i})}\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}\text{there exists some $W\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times\Theta_{i}}$ such that}\\
\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})=1~\text{($\forall\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}$) and}\\
\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}Q((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))=W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})~\text{($\forall\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}$)}\end{array}\right\}.

(6)

Lemma 3.1
.

The untruthful swap regret can be written as

R_{\mathsf{US},i}=\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Qx^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle x^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle.

Proof.

The expected reward for the algorithm is

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})\right]\right]=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i};a_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle x^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle.

Next, we express the competitor’s transformation that uses
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
as a linear transformation.
Given
\pi^{t}_{i}\in\Delta(A_{i})^{\Theta_{i}}
, the competitor’s expected reward in each round
t\in[T]
is

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi(\theta_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}))\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi(\theta_{i});a_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}x^{t}(\psi(\theta_{i}),a_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}))

\displaystyle=\left\langle Q_{\psi}x^{t},Q_{\phi}\bar{u}^{t}\right\rangle,

where
Q_{\psi},Q_{\phi}\in[0,1]^{(\Theta_{i}\times A_{i})\times(\Theta_{i}\times A_{i})}
are the matrices defined as follows.

•

The linear transformation
Q_{\psi}
for
x^{t}
is defined as the Kronecker product
Q_{\psi}=W_{\psi}\otimes I
of the zero-one stochastic matrix
W_{\psi}\in\{0,1\}^{\Theta_{i}\times\Theta_{i}}
, where
W_{\psi}(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})=1
if
\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\psi(\theta_{i})
and
0
otherwise, and the identity matrix
I\in\{0,1\}^{A_{i}\times A_{i}}
.

•

The linear transformation
Q_{\phi}
for
\bar{u}^{t}
is defined as the block diagonal matrix, where each block
Q_{\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)}\in\{0,1\}^{A_{i}\times A_{i}}
corresponding to
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
is the zero-one stochastic matrix defined by
Q_{\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)}(a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})=1
if
a^{\prime}_{i}=\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i})
and
0
otherwise.

Since
\left\langle Q_{\psi}x^{t},Q_{\phi}\bar{u}^{t}\right\rangle=\left\langle\left(Q_{\phi}^{\top}Q_{\psi}\right)x^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\right\rangle
, the competitor can be interpreted as transforming
x^{t}
by using matrix
Q_{\phi}^{\top}Q_{\psi}
, which we denote by
Q_{\psi,\phi}
.
Hence, the set of all possible transformations for the competitor is

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\left\{Q_{\psi,\phi}\;\middle|\;\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i},\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}\right\}.

Next, we prove that
\mathcal{Q}
equals the convex hull of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.
For each
\psi
and
\phi
, by a simple calculation, we can see that each entry is
Q_{\psi,\phi}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))=1
if
\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\psi(\theta_{i})
and
a_{i}=\phi(\theta_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})
, and
0
otherwise.
All the constraints (
6
) for
\mathcal{Q}
are satisfied by
Q_{\psi,\phi}\in\mathcal{Q}
for any
\psi
and
\phi
.
The convex hull is therefore a subset of
\mathcal{Q}
.

Conversely, we show that any
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
can be expressed as a convex combination of
Q_{\psi,\phi}
.
Fix any
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
.
We can regard
Q
as a block matrix with block
Q_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}\in[0,1]^{A_{i}\times A_{i}}
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
From the definition of
\mathcal{Q}
, there exists some stochastic matrices
W\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times\Theta_{i}}
and
\tilde{Q}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}\in[0,1]^{A_{i}\times A_{i}}
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
such that
Q_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top}=W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})\tilde{Q}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
Since any stochastic matrix is a convex combination of zero-one stochastic matrices, there exists some distribution
\gamma
over all possible
\psi
such that
W=\sum_{\psi}\gamma(\psi)W_{\psi}
.
Similarly, for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, there exists some distribution
\kappa_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}
over all possible
\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)
such that
\tilde{Q}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}=\sum_{\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)}\kappa_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}(\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot))Q_{\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)}
.
Each entry of
Q
is

\displaystyle Q((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))

\displaystyle=W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})\tilde{Q}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}(a^{\prime}_{i},a_{i})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\psi}\gamma(\psi)W_{\psi}(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})\right)\left(\sum_{\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)}\kappa_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}(\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot))Q_{\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)}(a^{\prime}_{i},a_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\psi}\gamma(\psi)\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\psi(\theta_{i})\}}\right)\left(\sum_{\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)}\kappa_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}(\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot))\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}=\phi(\theta_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\psi\sim\gamma}\left(\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\psi(\theta_{i})\right)\Pr_{\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)\sim\kappa_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}}\left(a_{i}=\phi(\theta_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\right).

We consider a distribution that generates
\psi
and
\phi
with probability
\gamma({\psi})\prod_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\kappa_{\theta_{i},\psi(\theta_{i})}(\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot))
.
That is,
\psi
is generated from
\gamma
and then each
\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)
is generated from
\kappa_{\theta_{i},\psi(\theta_{i})}
independently for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
The expected value of each entry according to this distribution is

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\psi,\phi}\left[Q_{\psi,\phi}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\psi,\phi}\left(\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\psi(\theta_{i}),a_{i}=\phi(\theta_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\psi\sim\gamma}\left(\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\psi(\theta_{i})\right)\Pr_{\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)\sim\kappa_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}}\left(a_{i}=\phi(\theta_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=Q((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})).

Since every
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
can be expressed as a convex combination of some matrices in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, we can see that
\mathcal{Q}
is a subset of the convex hull of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.
We conclude that
\mathcal{Q}
equals the convex hull of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

The competitor’s expected reward is

\displaystyle\max_{\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}}\max_{\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a_{i}\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi(\theta_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}))\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\max_{\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}}\max_{\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\langle Q_{\psi,\phi}x^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Qx^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle,

where the last equality holds because
\langle Qx^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle
is linear in terms of
Q
, and
\mathcal{Q}
is the convex hull of
Q_{\psi,\phi}
for all
\psi
and
\phi
.
∎

3.2
Reduction to online linear optimization

We apply the framework developed by
Gordon et al. 2008
that reduces
\Phi
-regret minimization to fixed point computation and online linear optimization.
First, we show that each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
has a fixed point in
\mathcal{X}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For every
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, there exists
x\in\mathcal{X}
such that
Qx=x
.

Proof.

We prove this lemma by using Brouwer’s fixed point theorem, which claims that any continuous function that maps a compact convex set to itself has a fixed point.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is a product of probability simplices, it is compact and convex.
In addition,
Q
is continuous since it is a matrix.
Thus it is sufficient to prove that
Q
maps every
x\in\mathcal{X}
to a vector in
\mathcal{X}
.
Let
x\in\mathcal{X}
be an arbitrary vector.
Let
x_{\theta_{i}}
be the block of
x
corresponding to
\theta_{i}
and
Q_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}
be the block of
Q
corresponding to
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}
.
Then
x_{\theta_{i}}\in\Delta(A_{i})
is a stochastic vector for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
From the definition of
\mathcal{Q}
, there exists some stochastic matrices
W\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times\Theta_{i}}
and
\tilde{Q}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}\in[0,1]^{A_{i}\times A_{i}}
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}
such that
Q_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top}=W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})\tilde{Q}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
Since
\tilde{Q}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top}x_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}\in\Delta(A_{i})
is also a stochastic vector, each block of
Qx
corresponding to
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
is

\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}Q_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}x_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}=\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})\left(\tilde{Q}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top}x_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}\right).

This is a convex sum of stochastic vectors, hence a stochastic vector in
\Delta(A_{i})
.
This implies
Qx\in\mathcal{X}
for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
From Brouwer’s fixed point theorem, there exists a fixed point
x\in\mathcal{X}
such that
Qx=x
.
∎

Next, we show that this fixed point can be obtained by eigenvector computation.
As described later, all the subroutines are variants of the multiplicative weights, and therefore, all entries of their outputs are positive.
We can assume that each entry of
Q^{t}
, which is computed as a product of the subroutines’ outputs, is positive for every
t\in[T]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

If all entries of
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
are positive, then we can compute
x\in\mathcal{X}
such that
Qx=x
in time polynomial in
|\Theta_{i}|
and
|A_{i}|
.

Proof.

We apply the Perron–Frobenius theorem, which claims that any positive matrix has an eigenvalue whose eigenspace is
1
-dimensional, and any positive eigenvector is an eigenvector corresponding to this eigenvalue.
From
Lemma

3.2
, there exists some
x\in\mathcal{X}
such that
Qx=x
, which implies that
Q
has eigenvector
1
, and
x
is its corresponding eigenvector.
Since
Q
is a positive matrix and
x
is a non-negative non-zero vector,
Qx=x
is also a positive vector.
From the Perron–Frobenius thereom, the eigenspace corresponding to eigenvalue
1
is
1
-dimensional and contains only a multiple of
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
We can obtain
x\in\mathcal{X}
by computing an eigendecomposition of
Q
and appropriately scaling the eigenvector corresponding to eigenvalue
1
.
Since
Q
is a
|\Theta_{i}||A_{i}|\times|\Theta_{i}||A_{i}|
matrix, its eigendecomposition can be computed in
O(|\Theta_{i}|^{\omega}|A_{i}|^{\omega})
time, where
\omega
is the exponent of matrix multiplication.
∎

By using this fixed point computation, we can solve untruthful swap regret minimization with a subroutine for online linear optimization with decision space
\mathcal{Q}
.
Let
Q^{t}\in\mathcal{Q}
be the subroutine’s output in round
t\in[T]
.
Then compute a fixed point
x^{t}\in\mathcal{Q}
that satisfies
Q^{t}x^{t}=x^{t}
, and let
x^{t}
be the decision for round
t
.
For the subroutine, feed reward
U^{t}\in[0,1]^{(\Theta_{i}\times A_{i})\times(\Theta_{i}\times A_{i})}
defined by
U^{t}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))=\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})x^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
.
We define external regret for this subroutine as

R^{T}_{\mathcal{Q}}=\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q,U^{t}\rangle-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q^{t},U^{t}\rangle.

Then we show that untruthful swap regret
R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}
equals this subroutine’s external regret
R^{T}_{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}=R^{T}_{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Proof.

From the definition of
x^{t}
, it holds that
Q^{t}x^{t}=x^{t}
for each
t\in[T]
.
From the definition of
U^{t}
, the algorithm’s expected reward is

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q^{t},U^{t}\rangle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\langle Q^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\left(x^{t}\right)^{\top}\right\rangle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\langle Q^{t}x^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\right\rangle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\langle x^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\right\rangle,

where the last equality is due to
Q^{t}x^{t}=x^{t}
for each
t\in[T]
.
Similarly, the competitor’s expected reward is

\displaystyle\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q,U^{t}\rangle=\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\langle Q,\bar{u}^{t}\left(x^{t}\right)^{\top}\right\rangle=\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\langle Qx^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\right\rangle.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle R^{T}_{\mathcal{Q}}=\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q,U^{t}\rangle-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q^{t},U^{t}\rangle=\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Qx^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle x^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle=R_{\mathsf{US},i},

where the last equality is due to
Lemma

3.1
.
∎

3.3
Decomposition into small external regret minimization problems

Next, we reduce online linear optimization with decision space
\mathcal{Q}
to small external regret minimization problems.
For each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
, we use a subroutine
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}
with decision space
A_{i}
.
Moreover, for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, we use a subroutine
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i}}
with decision space
\Theta_{i}
.
In total, we use
|A_{i}||\Theta_{i}|^{2}+|\Theta_{i}|
subroutines.

The reduction proceeds as follows.
Let
y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}\in\Delta(A_{i})
be the output of
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}
and
w^{t}_{\theta_{i}}\in\Delta(\Theta_{i})
the output of
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i}}
in round
t\in[T]
.
According to these outputs, we set
Q^{t}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))=w^{t}_{\theta_{i}}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}(a_{i})
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
.
Note that this
Q^{t}
is contained in
\mathcal{Q}
, which can be checked by setting
W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})=w^{t}_{\theta_{i}}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.

In each round
t\in[T]
, based on the observed reward
U^{t}=\bar{u}^{t}(x^{t})^{\top}
, we feed the reward for each subroutine as follows.
For each
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}
, the reward for decision
a_{i}\in A_{i}
is
x^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})
.
The external regret
R_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}^{T}
for
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}
is defined as

R_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}^{T}=\max_{a^{*}_{i}\in A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a^{*}_{i})-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}(a_{i})x^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i}).

For each
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i}}
, the reward for decision
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
is
\sum_{a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}(a_{i})x^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})
.
The external regret
R_{\theta_{i}}^{T}
for
\mathcal{E}_{\theta_{i}}
is defined as

\displaystyle R_{\theta_{i}}^{T}

\displaystyle=\max_{\theta^{*}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{*}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}(a_{i})x^{t}(\theta^{*}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})

\displaystyle-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}w_{\theta_{i}}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})\sum_{a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}(a_{i})x^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i}).

The external regret for online linear optimization with decision space
\mathcal{Q}
is bounded by the sum of the external regrets for these subroutines as follows.

Lemma 3.5
.

R^{T}_{\mathcal{Q}}\leq\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}R^{T}_{\theta_{i}}+\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\max_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}R^{T}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}.

Proof.

Recall that
U^{t}=\bar{u}^{t}(x^{t})^{\top}
.
Since each extreme point of
\mathcal{Q}
corresponds to a matrix
(Q_{\phi}^{\top}Q_{\psi})
for some
\psi
and
\phi
, we obtain

\displaystyle\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q,U^{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Qx^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\max_{\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}}\max_{\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q_{\psi}x^{t},Q_{\phi}\bar{u}^{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\max_{\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}}\max_{\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{t}(\psi(\theta_{i}),a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},\phi(\theta_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\max_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}\max_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\max_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}\left\{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}(a_{i})x^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})+R^{T}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}\right\}

(due to the definition of
R^{T}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}
)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\left\{\max_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}(a_{i})x^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})+\max_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}R^{T}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\left\{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}w^{t}_{\theta_{i}}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}y^{t}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}(a_{i})x^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\bar{u}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})+R^{T}_{\theta_{i}}+\max_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}R^{T}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}\right\}

(due to the definition of
R^{T}_{\theta_{i}}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q^{t},U^{t}\rangle+\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}R^{T}_{\theta_{i}}+\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\max_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}R^{T}_{\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}},

(due to the definition of
Q^{t}
and
U^{t}
)

which yields an upper bound on
R^{T}_{\mathcal{Q}}=\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q,U^{t}\rangle-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle Q^{t},U^{t}\rangle
.
∎

To obtain an upper bound on untruthful swap regret, we use the following upper bounds for external regret minimization.
These algorithms are based on the multiplicative weights, and their output is always a positive vector.

Theorem 3.6

(
(
Cesa-Bianchi and Lugosi 2006
, Theorem 2.2)
)
.

For an online learning problem with rewards in
[0,1]
, there exists an algorithm such that its external regret is bounded above by
\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}T\log d}
, where
d
is the number of possible decisions.

Theorem 3.7

(
(
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 1997
, Theorem 4.6.3)
)
.

For an online learning problem with rewards in
[0,1]
, there exists an algorithm such that its external regret is bounded above by
6\sqrt{u^{*}\log d}+2\log d
, where
d
is the number of possible decisions and
u^{*}
is the sum of rewards for an optimal fixed decision over
T
rounds.
13
13

13

For simplicity, the constants are not optimized. We can instead use an algorithm with adaptive step size proposed by
Auer et al. 2002
.

These three-step reduction yields the algorithm described in
Algorithm

3
.
Finally, we obtain an upper bound on untruthful swap regret as follows.

Theorem 3.8
.

The untruthful swap regret of
Algorithm

3
is bounded as

R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}T\log|\Theta_{i}|}+6\sqrt{T|A_{i}|\log|A_{i}|}+2|A_{i}|\log|A_{i}|.

Proof.

From
Lemmas

3.4
and
3.5
, the untruthful swap regret of
Algorithm

3
is bounded as
