\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{t\sim\mathcal{T}_{b},\xi_{b}}\left[\left|X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right|\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]\geq c.

By applying
Lemma

4.5
, we obtain

\Pr_{t\sim\mathcal{T}_{b},\xi_{b}}\left(\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{c^{2}}{4}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)=\Pr_{t\sim\mathcal{T}_{b},\xi_{b}}\left(\left|X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right|\geq\frac{c}{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)\geq\frac{c}{2}.

We thus obtain

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]=L\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{t\sim\mathcal{T}_{b},\xi_{b}}\left[\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]=L\cdot\frac{c}{2}\cdot\frac{c^{2}}{4}\geq\frac{c^{3}L}{8}.

(24)

We show that a stochastic process
(D_{k}^{t})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{b}\colon t^{\prime}\leq t}\left(X_{0}^{t^{\prime}}-X_{1}^{t^{\prime}}\right)^{2}
for
t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}
is a martingale.
We denote by
\mathcal{F}_{t}
all the events that happen until the end of time round
t
.
Conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
, the expected difference is

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[(D_{k}^{t})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{b}\colon t^{\prime}\leq t}\left(X_{0}^{t^{\prime}}-X_{1}^{t^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]-\left\{(D_{k}^{t-1})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{b}\colon t^{\prime}\leq t-1}\left(X_{0}^{t^{\prime}}-X_{1}^{t^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[(D_{k}^{t})^{2}-(D_{k}^{t-1})^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[(D_{k}^{t}-D_{k}^{t-1})(D_{k}^{t}+D_{k}^{t-1})\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[(D_{k}^{t}-D_{k}^{t-1})(2D_{k}^{t-1}+(D_{k}^{t}-D_{k}^{t-1}))\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=2D_{k}^{t-1}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[D_{k}^{t}-D_{k}^{t-1}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[(D_{k}^{t}-D_{k}^{t-1})^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=0+\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}

(since (
20
))

\displaystyle=0.

We define the random variable
t^{*}\in\mathcal{T}_{b}
such that

t^{*}=\begin{cases}\min\left\{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}\;\middle|\;|D_{k}^{t}|\geq\frac{\sqrt{c^{3}L}}{4}\right\}&\text{if $\mathcal{F}$ holds}\\
{t_{\mathrm{max}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

is the time round when
|D_{k}^{t}|
exceeds
\frac{\sqrt{c^{3}L}}{4}
for the first time if
\mathcal{F}
holds.
Since the stopping time
t^{*}
is bounded above by
{t_{\mathrm{max}}}
, we can apply Doob’s optional stopping theorem to the martingale defined above and obtain

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[(D_{k}^{t^{*}})^{2}-\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}\colon t\leq t^{*}}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]=0.

Since
\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}\colon t>t^{*}}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}
is
0
if
\mathcal{F}
does not hold and bounded above by
L
if
\mathcal{F}
holds, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}\colon t\leq t^{*}}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}\colon t>t^{*}}\left(X_{0}^{t}-X_{1}^{t}\right)^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[(D_{k}^{t^{*}})^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]+\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)L

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[(D_{k}^{t^{*}})^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},\mathcal{F}\right]+\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}^{c}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[(D_{k}^{t^{*}})^{2}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},\mathcal{F}^{c}\right]+\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)L

\displaystyle\leq\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)\left(\frac{\sqrt{c^{3}L}}{4}+1\right)^{2}+\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}^{c}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)\left(\frac{\sqrt{c^{3}L}}{4}\right)^{2}+\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)L

\displaystyle\leq 4L\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)+\frac{c^{3}L}{16},

where we used
c\leq 1
,
\sqrt{L}\leq L
,
L\geq 1
, and
\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}^{c}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)\leq 1
for the last inequality.
Combining it with (
24
), we obtain

\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)\geq\frac{c^{3}L/8-c^{3}L/16}{4L}=\frac{c^{3}}{64}.

(25)

Plugging (
23
) and (
25
) into (
22
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{I}_{p,0}\\
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p,0}\end{subarray}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{I}_{p,1}\\
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p,1}\end{subarray}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}Y_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}^{t}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p,0}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}Z_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}^{t}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p,1}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}Z_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}^{t}\right]\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]

(since (
18
))

\displaystyle=\frac{L}{2}+\frac{1}{2^{B-b+1}}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{B-b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[h_{k}(\xi_{b})D_{k}^{t_{\mathrm{max}}}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]

(since (
19
) and (
21
))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{L}{2}+\frac{1}{2^{B-b+1}}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{B-b}}\frac{1}{2}\Pr_{\xi_{b}}\left(\mathcal{F}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right)\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[D_{k}^{t_{\mathrm{max}}}\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},\mathcal{F}_{+}\right]

(since (
22
))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{L}{2}+\frac{1}{2^{B-b+1}}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{B-b}}\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{c^{3}}{64}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{c^{3}L}}{4}

(since (
23
) and (
25
))

\displaystyle=\frac{L}{2}+2^{-10}c^{9/2}\sqrt{L},

which completes the proof.
∎

4.4
Proof of lower bound

Finally, we prove the theorem using
Lemmas

4.6
,
4.4
and
4.7
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

Since the rewards for type
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}
are determined by independent Bernoulli random variables
\xi
, and therefore the expected reward obtained by the algorithm for each round is equal to
1/2
.
Formally, since the algorithm’s decision
\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i})
is independent from
u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha_{0})=1-u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha_{1})=\xi(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i})(t)
, the expected reward is

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i};\alpha_{0})\xi(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i})(t)+(1-\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i};\alpha_{0}))(1-\xi(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i})(t))\right]

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i};\alpha_{0})\cdot\frac{1}{2}+(1-\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i};\alpha_{0}))\cdot\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}.

We consider
\phi
in the definition of untruthful swap regret such that
\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)
is the identity map for every
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
By considering only the types in
\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\max_{\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi(\theta_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},\alpha)\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\max_{\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}}\frac{1}{|\Theta_{i}|}\sum_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\max_{\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}}\frac{1}{|\Theta_{i}|}\sum_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]-\frac{1}{|\Theta_{i}|}\sum_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\max_{\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}}\frac{1}{|\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}|}\sum_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]-\frac{T}{4},

where the last equality is due to
|\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}|=|\Theta_{i}|/2
.
By using
\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}
defined in
Section

4.2
for each
\zeta
and
\xi
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\frac{1}{|\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}|}\sum_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]-\frac{T}{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\in[B]}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\frac{1}{|\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}|}\sum_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]-\frac{L}{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{B}{2}\left\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim[B]}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\right]\right]-\frac{L}{2}\right\}.

(26)

For each
b\in[B]
, since
(\mathcal{J}_{p})_{p\in\{0,1\}^{b-1}}
is the equally sized partition of
\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, each term can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{p\sim\{0,1\}^{b-1}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\\
p\sim\{0,1\}^{b-1}\end{subarray}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\\
p\sim\{0,1\}^{b-1}\end{subarray}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p,0}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\right.\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.\left.+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p,1}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\\
p\sim\{0,1\}^{b-1}\end{subarray}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p,0}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b}\right]\right.\right.\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.\left.+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p,1}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b}\right]\right]\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]\right].

Conditioned on
\zeta_{b}
and
\xi_{b}
, all of
\mathcal{J}_{p,0}
,
\mathcal{J}_{p,1}
,
(\pi_{i}^{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}
, and
(u_{i}^{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}
are deterministic.
For taking the expectation with respect to
\zeta
and
\xi
conditioned on
\zeta_{b}
and
\xi_{b}
,
we need to consider only the randomness of
\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i})
.
From
Lemma

4.6
,
\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i})
is distributed uniformly over
\mathcal{I}_{p,0}
for each
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{p,0}
.
Similarly,
\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i})
is distributed uniformly over
\mathcal{I}_{p,1}
for each
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{p,1}
.
Therefore, the above term can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\\
p\sim\{0,1\}^{b-1}\end{subarray}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{I}_{p,0}\\
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p,0}\end{subarray}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{I}_{p,0}\\
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\mathcal{J}_{p,1}\end{subarray}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]\right].

For each
b\in[B]
,
\zeta_{b}
,
\xi_{b-1}
, and
p\in\{0,1\}^{b-1}
, let
\mathcal{F}_{b,\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p}
be the event that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{b}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\left|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\mathcal{I}_{p,0}}\left[\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i};\alpha_{0})\right]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\mathcal{I}_{p,1}}\left[\pi_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i};\alpha_{0})\right]\right|\;\middle|\;\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1}\right]\geq\frac{L}{8}.

Toward a contradiction, assume
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}\right]<2^{-28}\sqrt{T\log_{2}|\Theta_{i}|}
.
From
Lemma

4.4
, we have
\Pr_{b,\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p}(\mathcal{F}_{b,\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p})\geq 1/8
.
Next, we apply
Lemma

4.7
to each
b
,
\zeta_{b}
,
\xi_{b-1}
and
p
.
If
\mathcal{F}_{b,\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p}
holds, we apply
Lemma

4.7
with
c=1/8
.
If
\mathcal{F}_{b,\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p}
does not hold, since the assumption of
Lemma

4.7
always holds for
c=0
, we apply
Lemma

4.7
with
c=0
.
Then we obtain a lower bound on the above term as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim[B]}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sim\Theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\left[\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{b}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{i}^{t}(\psi^{\prime}_{\zeta,\xi}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}))}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\alpha)\right]\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\sim[B],\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p\sim\{0,1\}^{b-1}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{b,\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p}\right)\left(\frac{L}{2}+2^{-24}\sqrt{L}\right)+\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\sim[B],\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p\sim\{0,1\}^{b-1}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathcal{F}^{c}_{b,\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p}\right)\frac{L}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{L}{2}+\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\sim[B],\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p\sim\{0,1\}^{b-1}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{b,\zeta_{b},\xi_{b-1},p}\right)\cdot 2^{-24}\sqrt{L}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{L}{2}+2^{-27}\sqrt{L}.

Finally, substituting this into (
26
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{B}{2}\cdot 2^{-27}\sqrt{L}

\displaystyle=2^{-28}\cdot B\cdot\sqrt{T/B}

(since
L=T/B
)

\displaystyle=2^{-28}\sqrt{TB}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{-28}\sqrt{T\log_{2}|\Theta_{i}|},

which contradicts the assumption

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta,\xi}\left[R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}\right]<2^{-28}\sqrt{T\log_{2}|\Theta_{i}|}
.
Then there exist some
\zeta
and
\xi
such that for the problem instance with parameter
\zeta
and
\xi
, it holds
R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}\geq 2^{-28}\sqrt{T\log_{2}|\Theta_{i}|}
.
∎

5
Smoothness and price of anarchy

In this section, we show lower bounds on the price of anarchy (POA) in Bayesian games via the smoothness assumption.
As in existing studies
(
Roughgarden 2015b
;
Syrgkanis 2012
;
Syrgkanis and Tardos 2013
)
, we make the following two assumptions throughout this section.

•

Assumption 1: the prior distribution
\rho\in\Delta(\Theta)
is a product distribution, that is, there exists some
\rho_{i}\in\Delta(\Theta_{i})
for each
i\in N
such that
\rho(\theta)=\prod_{i\in N}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})
.

•

Assumption 2: the value of
v_{i}(\theta;a)
does not depend on
\theta_{-i}
.
Under this assumption, we write
v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)
in place of
v_{i}(\theta;a)
by abuse of notation.

These assumptions naturally hold when type
\theta_{i}
represents a preference or attribute of each player
i\in N
.
We define the price of anarchy as follows.

Definition 5.1

(Price of anarchy of Bayesian games)
.

Given an equilibrium class
\Pi\subseteq\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
, its price of anarchy is defined by

{\mathsf{POA}}_{\Pi}=\frac{\displaystyle\inf_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right]}{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\max_{a\in A}v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]},

where
v_{\mathsf{SW}}\colon\Theta\times A\to[0,1]
is the social welfare function.

Most of the existing studies considered the case where
\Pi
is Bayes Nash equilibria.
There are several exceptions
(
Caragiannis et al. 2015
;
Hartline et al. 2015
)
in which
\Pi
is some class of Bayes coarse correlated equilibria, but their applications are limited (see
Section

1.3
).
Throughout this section,
\Pi
is the set of (non-approximate) communication equilibria with strategy representability.
As described in
Sections

2
and
3
, an equilibrium in this class can be efficiently computed by simulating no-untruthful-swap-regret dynamics.

We prepare a lemma that is commonly used in all three cases.
The proof is based on the ones provided by
Roughgarden 2015b
;
Syrgkanis 2012
;
Syrgkanis and Tardos 2013
, but we here extend them from Bayes Nash equilibria to communication equilibria with strategy representability.
We use the characterization of this class given in
Proposition

2.18
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose that a Bayesian game satisfies Assumptions 1 and 2.
Fix any
i\in N
.
Given any
a_{i,\theta,a_{i}}^{*}\in A_{i}
for each
\theta\in\Theta
and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a^{*}_{i,\theta,a_{i}},a_{-i})\right]\right]\right],

where
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
is any communication equilibrium with strategy representability.

Proof.

Let
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
be any communication equilibrium with strategy representability.
From
Proposition

2.18
, there exists
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
that satisfies
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
and (

IC
ComSR

) for each
i\in N
,
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
, and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
Recall that
\eta\colon\Delta(S)\to\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
is defined as
(\eta(\sigma))(\theta;a)=\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}(s(\theta)=a)
for each
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
(see
Section

2.1
).
Since
\eta(\sigma)=\pi
, the expected payoff for player
i
in this equilibrium is

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s(\theta))\right]\right].

(27)

Next, we apply (

IC
ComSR

) for each
i\in N
.
Fix any
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
.
If we set
\psi(\theta_{i})=\theta^{\prime}_{i}
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i})=a^{*}_{i,(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{-i}),a_{i}}
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
, then (

IC
ComSR

) implies

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a^{*}_{i,(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{-i}),s_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})},s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right].

By taking the expectation over
\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho
, we obtain

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a^{*}_{i,(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{-i}),s_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})},s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]\right].

Since
\rho
is a product distribution, we can swap
\theta_{-i}
and
\theta^{\prime}_{-i}
on the right-hand side and obtain

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a^{*}_{i,\theta,s_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})},s_{-i}(\theta^{\prime}_{-i}))\right]\right]\right].

By plugging this inequality into (
27
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a^{*}_{i,\theta,s_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})},s_{-i}(\theta^{\prime}_{-i}))\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a^{*}_{i,\theta,a_{i}},a_{-i})\right]\right]\right],

where the equality holds since the distribution of
s(\theta^{\prime})
equals that of
a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})
due to
\pi=\eta(\sigma)
.
∎

5.1
POA bounds for the sum of payoffs

First, we assume that the social welfare is the sum of all players’ payoffs, i.e.,
v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)=\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)
.
Under this assumption,
Syrgkanis 2012
proved a POA lower bound with (unconditional) smoothness, and
Syrgkanis and Tardos 2013
then extended it to a weaker version of smoothness, called
conditional smoothness
.

Definition 5.3

(
(\lambda,\mu)
-Conditional smoothness of Bayesian games
(
Syrgkanis and Tardos 2013
)
)
.

A Bayesian game is
(\lambda,\mu)
-conditionally smooth
if
there exists some
a^{*}_{i,\theta,a_{i}}\in A_{i}
for each
i\in N
,
\theta\in\Theta
, and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
such that

\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}(\theta_{i};a^{*}_{i,\theta,a_{i}},a_{-i})\geq\lambda\max_{a^{\prime}\in A}v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a^{\prime})-\mu v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)

holds for any
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
.

Syrgkanis and Tardos 2013
introduced conditional smoothness for analyzing the POA of Bayes Nash equilibria.
We extend their POA bounds to communication equilibria with strategy representability.
Note that if (unconditional) smoothness is assumed,
Lemma

5.2
can be proved for a broader class of Bayes coarse correlated equilibria.
However, even under the (unconditional) smoothness assumption, the following proof requires both the incentive constraints for communication equilibria and strategy representability.

Theorem 5.4
.

Assume that the social welfare is the sum of all players’ payoffs.
For any
(\mu,\lambda)
-conditionally smooth Bayesian game satisfying Assumptions 1 and 2, the price of anarchy of communication equilibria with strategy representability is at least
\lambda/(1+\mu)
.

Proof.

Let
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
be any communication equilibrium with strategy representability.
By taking the summation of
Lemma

5.2
over
i\in N
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in N}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in N}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};a^{*}_{i,\theta,a_{i}},a_{-i})\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})}\left[\lambda\max_{a^{\prime}\in A}v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a^{\prime})-\mu v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in A}v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a^{\prime})\right]-\mu\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\right].

(28)

As in the proof of
Lemma

5.2
, we use
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
defined in
Proposition

2.18
.
By using the assumption of
v_{\mathsf{SW}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;s(\theta^{\prime}))\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}(\theta_{i};s(\theta^{\prime}))\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in N}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s(\theta^{\prime}))\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in N}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]\right],

(29)

where in the last equality,
\theta^{\prime}_{-i}
is replaced with
\theta_{-i}
since
\rho
is a product distribution.
Next, we apply (

IC
ComSR

) for each
i\in N
.
Fix any
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
If we set
\psi(\theta_{i})=\theta^{\prime}_{i}
for any
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i})=a_{i}
for any
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
, then (

IC
ComSR

) implies

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right].

By taking the expectation over
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\sim\rho_{i}
, we obtain

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right]\right].

By plugging this inequality into (
29
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\right]\leq\sum_{i\in N}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta_{i};s(\theta))\right]\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right].

From (
28
), we obtain

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\geq\lambda\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in A}v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a^{\prime})\right]-\mu\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right],

and then rearranging terms leads to a desired lower bound on the price of anarchy.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

In the proof of
Theorem

5.4
(and also in the proof of
Lemma

5.2
), we use (

IC
ComSR

) only for constant map
\psi
.
We can prove the same POA bound for the equilibrium concept with
\psi
restricted to constant maps, which is broader than communication equilibria with strategy representability.
However, since each player
i\in N
knows their type
\theta_{i}
by definition, the equilibrium concept in which each player ignores their type is artificial.

5.2
POA bounds for conditionally smooth mechanisms

Next, we consider applications to mechanism design.
Since it is not the focus of this paper, we do not introduce mechanism design in details.
See, e.g.,
Nisan et al. 2007
or
Hartline 2013
for basics of mechanism design and
Syrgkanis and Tardos 2013
for smooth mechanisms.

A mechanism (for auctions) is regarded as a function that maps type reports (bids)
a\in A
from players to an allocation and payment.
Let
X_{i}
be the set of allocation for player
i\in N
and
X\subseteq\prod_{i\in N}X_{i}
the set of all possible allocations.
An allocation function
f_{i}\colon A\to X_{i}
for each
i\in N
determines the allocation for player
i
based on the bids
a\in A
.
We can consider the process of mechanisms as a game in which each player decides a bid
a_{i}\in A_{i}
and then obtains a payoff based on the allocation and the payment.
We assume that each player’s utility function
v_{i}
is
quasilinear
, i.e., there exist some valuation function
v_{i}^{+}\colon\Theta_{i}\times X_{i}\to[0,1]
and payment function
v_{i}^{-}\colon A\to[0,1]
such that

v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)=v_{i}^{+}(\theta_{i};f_{i}(a))-v_{i}^{-}(a).

An important assumption here is the payment function depends only on
a
, not on
\theta_{i}
.
This assumption naturally holds because the payment is determined by the mechanism that does not know each player’s type.
We also assume that the payoff of each player is always non-negative, i.e.,
v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)\geq 0
for any
a\in A
.
This should hold because each player can withdraw from the mechanism if the payoff is negative.
The social welfare function is defined as
v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)=\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}^{+}(\theta_{i};f_{i}(a))
for each
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
.

Syrgkanis and Tardos 2013
proved that various simple Bayesian mechanisms and their simultaneous/sequential compositions are conditionally smooth.

Definition 5.6

(
(\lambda,\mu)
-Conditional smoothness of Bayesian mechanisms
(
Syrgkanis and Tardos 2013
)
)
.

A Bayesian mechanism is
(\lambda,\mu)
-conditionally smooth
if
there exists some
a^{*}_{i,\theta,a_{i}}\in A_{i}
for each
i\in N
,
\theta\in\Theta
, and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
such that

\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}(\theta_{i};a^{*}_{i,\theta,a_{i}},a_{-i})\geq\lambda\max_{x\in X}\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}^{+}(\theta_{i};x_{i})-\mu\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}^{-}(a).

holds for any
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
.

Syrgkanis and Tardos 2013
proved a
\lambda/\max\{1,\mu\}
lower bound on the POA of Bayes Nash equilibria with this assumption.
We extend it to communication equilibria with strategy representability.

Theorem 5.7
.

For any
(\mu,\lambda)
-conditionally smooth mechanism satisfying Assumptions 1 and 2, the price of anarchy of communication equilibria with strategy representability is at least
\lambda/\max\{1,\mu\}
.

Proof.

Let
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
be any communication equilibrium with strategy representability.
By summing
Lemma

5.2
for each
i\in N
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})}\left[\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}(\theta_{i};a^{*}_{i,\theta,a_{i}},a_{-i})\right]\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})}\left[\lambda\max_{x\in X}\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}^{+}(\theta_{i};x_{i})-\mu\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}^{-}(a)\right]\right]\right]

(due to conditional smoothness)

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\max_{x\in X}\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}^{+}(\theta_{i};x_{i})\right]-\mu\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}^{-}(a)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\max_{a\in A}v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]-\mu\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}^{-}(a)\right]\right].

Since
v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)=v_{i}^{+}(\theta_{i};f_{i}(a))-v_{i}^{-}(a)
for each
i\in N
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\geq\lambda\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\max_{a\in A}v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]+(1-\mu)\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}^{-}(a)\right]\right].

If
\mu\leq 1
, the second term on the right-hand side is non-negative, and therefore, the POA is at least
\lambda
.
If
\mu>1
, since
v_{i}(\theta_{i};a)=v_{i}^{+}(\theta_{i};f_{i}(a))-v_{i}^{-}(a)\geq 0
, the second term on the right-hand side is bounded below by
(1-\mu)\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;s(\theta))\right]\right]
, which implies a
\lambda/\mu
lower bound on the POA.
∎

Syrgkanis and Tardos 2013
also proved a similar result for weak smoothness and budget constraints.
We can generalize our proof to these settings and obtain POA bounds for more various mechanisms.
These results yield lower bounds on the POA of communication equilibria with strategy representability for the simultaneous and sequential composition of first-price, second-price, all-pay, and many other auctions.

Remark 5.8
.

In applications to auctions, a type
\theta_{i}
represents a valuation of an item for player
i
, and an action
a_{i}\in A_{i}
represents a bid for this good.
The sets of possible values
\Theta_{i}
and
A_{i}
are usually continuous intervals.
Our proposed algorithm for computing an equilibrium does not assume any structure for
\Theta_{i}
and
A_{i}
and requires polynomial time in
|\Theta_{i}|
and
|A_{i}|
, which might be prohibitive.
A naive solution to this issue is to discretize
\Theta_{i}
and
A_{i}
.
Another possible solution is to design an efficient algorithm using the continuous structure of
\Theta_{i}
and
A_{i}
, which is left for future work.

5.3
POA bounds for type-dependent action sets

We consider the case where the set of actions for each player changes depending on their type.
Here we briefly describe how we can extend the concepts introduced so far and then provide a lower bound on the POA.

The set of actions for type
\theta_{i}
is restricted as
A_{i,\theta_{i}}\subseteq A_{i}
for each
i\in N
and
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
The set of strategies
S_{i}\subseteq A_{i}^{\Theta_{i}}
is also restricted so that
s_{i}(\theta_{i})\in A_{i,\theta_{i}}
holds for each
i\in N
,
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, and
s_{i}\in S_{i}
.
The utility function is defined as
v_{i}(\theta;\cdot)\colon\prod_{i\in N}A_{i,\theta_{i}}\to[0,1]
for each
i\in N
and
\theta\in\Theta
, but here we still assume that
v_{i}(\theta;a)=v_{i}(\theta^{\prime};a)
for any
a\in\prod_{i\in N}A_{i,\theta_{i}}\cap\prod_{i\in N}A_{i,\theta^{\prime}_{i}}
if
\theta_{i}=\theta^{\prime}_{i}
.
The definition of approximate communication equilibria is generalized as follows.

Definition 5.9

(
\epsilon
-Approximate communication equilibria for type-dependent action sets)
.

For any
\epsilon\geq 0
, a type-wise distribution
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
such that the support of
\pi(\theta)
is a subset of
\prod_{i\in N}A_{i,\theta_{i}}
for each
\theta\in\Theta
is an
\epsilon
-approximate communication equilibrium
if
for any
i\in N
,
\psi\colon\Theta_{i}\to\Theta_{i}
, and
\phi(\theta_{i},\cdot)\colon A_{i,\psi(\theta_{i})}\to A_{i,\theta_{i}}
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\psi(\theta_{i}),\theta_{-i})}\left[v_{i}(\theta;\phi(\theta_{i},a_{i}),a_{-i})\right]\right]-\epsilon.

The definition of strategy representability is also generalized with restricted strategy profiles
S=\prod_{i\in N}S_{i}
.
Our proposed dynamics and algorithm for minimizing untruthful swap regret are naturally extended to this setting.

Roughgarden 2015b
and
Syrgkanis 2012
assumed
universal smoothness
of Bayesian games with type-dependent action sets.
This condition holds in routing games
14
14

14

Precisely speaking, we need to consider the
minimization
version in which each player minimizes their cost instead of maximizing their payoff. A similar result can be obtained by the same proof.

(
Roughgarden 2015b
;
Syrgkanis 2012
)
and Bayesian effort games
(
Syrgkanis 2012
)
.
Here we consider its conditional version, which is an assumption weaker than universal smoothness.

Definition 5.10

(
(\lambda,\mu)
-Universal conditional smoothness of Bayesian games)
.

A Bayesian game is
(\lambda,\mu)
-universally conditionally smooth
if
there exists some
a^{*}_{i,\theta,a_{i}}\in A_{i,\theta_{i}}
for each
i\in N
,
\theta\in\Theta
, and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
such that

\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}(\theta;a^{*}_{i,\theta,a_{i}},a_{-i})\geq\lambda\max_{a^{\prime}\in\prod_{i\in N}A_{i,\theta_{i}}}v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a^{\prime})-\mu v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta^{\prime};a).

holds for any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
and
a\in A
with
a_{i}\in A_{i,\theta^{\prime}_{i}}
for each
i\in N
.

We can generalize
Lemma

5.2
to this setting and apply this smoothness inequality to its summation over
i\in N
.
We also assume that the social welfare is
payoff-dominating
, i.e.,
v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\geq\sum_{i\in N}v_{i}(\theta;a)
for each
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
such that
a_{i}\in A_{i,\theta_{i}}
for each
i\in N
.
Then we obtain

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\geq\lambda\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\max_{a^{\prime}\in\prod_{i\in N}A_{i,\theta_{i}}}v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta;a^{\prime})\right]-\mu\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})}\left[v_{\mathsf{SW}}(\theta^{\prime};a)\right]\right],

which leads a
\lambda/(1+\mu)
lower bound on the POA of communication equilibria with strategy representability.

6
Concluding remarks

We have studied the classes of Bayes correlated equilibria from the viewpoint of efficient computability and the price of anarchy.
We proposed dynamics converging to communication equilibria with strategy representability and showed how to simulate the dynamics efficiently by designing an algorithm for minimizing untruthful swap regret.
We also proved a lower bound on untruthful swap regret that matches the upper bound up to a multiplicative constant.
The price of anarchy of communication equilibria with strategy representability was analyzed via the smoothness argument that has been applied to Bayes Nash equilibria.

The problem of efficiently computing an approximate strategic-form correlated equilibrium is left unsolved.
An approach to this problem is to design an efficient algorithm for minimizing strategy swap regret.
Although the number of possible swaps
\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}
is doubly exponential, we can reduce this number by considering the simplicity of the intrinsic decision space
\mathcal{X}
.
In the opposite direction, it might be possible to prove a superpolynomial lower bound on the query complexity.
It is still difficult for us to judge which direction is more promising.

Another possible direction for future work is to investigate relations between communication equilibria and Bayesian mechanism design.
The only difference between them is whether a mediator sends recommendations to players or determines an outcome in a top-down fashion.
This combination may bring new possibilities for Bayesian mechanism design.
For example, we proved a POA lower bound for smooth Bayesian mechanisms, but its consequence is still unexplored.
A communication equilibrium of a mechanism provides another mechanism with a truthtelling equilibrium, but we do not know its appropriate interpretation.

Appendix A
On conditional independence property

In this section, we show the difference between the strategy representability and the conditional independence property.
The conditional independence property was mentioned by
Forges 1993
as an important property that holds for SFCEs and ANFCEs.

Definition A.1

(Conditional independence property)
.

Let
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
.
We consider a joint distribution in
\Delta(\Theta\times A)
that first generates
\theta\in\Theta
according to
\rho
and then generates
a\in A
according to
\pi(\theta)
.
We say
\pi
satisfies the
conditional independence property

15
15

15

It is originally defined as a property of a joint distribution in
\Delta(\Theta\times A)
, but we can consider a joint distribution in
\Delta(\Theta\times A)
and a type-wise distribution in
\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
interchangeably.
For each
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
, the corresponding joint distribution in
\Delta(\Theta\times A)
is uniquely determined.
Conversely, for any joint distribution whose marginal for
\theta
coincides with
\rho
, we can uniquely determine the corresponding distribution in
\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
.

if
a_{i}
is conditionally independent of
\theta_{-i}
given
\theta_{i}
for each
i\in N
.

As
Forges 1993
claimed, this property holds for any strategy-representable distribution.
Although it was not claimed explicitly, this property is not equivalent to the strategy representability.

Proposition A.2
.

If
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
is strategy-representable,
then
\pi
satisfies the conditional independence property.
On the other hand, there exists
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
that satisfies the conditional independence property but not the strategy representability.

Proof.

First, we show that the strategy representability implies the conditional independence property.
Assume there exists
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
such that
\pi(\theta;a)=\Pr_{s\sim\sigma}\left(s(\theta)=a\right)
for each
\theta\in\Theta
and
a\in A
.
To prove the conditional independence property, it is sufficient to prove the conditional independence of
a_{i}
and
\theta_{-i}
given
\theta_{i}
for any
i\in N
,
\theta\in\Theta
, and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
.
Using the strategy representability of
\pi
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho\\
a^{\prime}\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})\end{subarray}}(\theta^{\prime}=\theta,~a^{\prime}_{i}=a_{i})\Pr_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i})

\displaystyle=\left(\rho(\theta)\Pr_{a^{\prime}\sim\pi(\theta)}(a^{\prime}_{i}=a_{i})\right)\Pr_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i})

\displaystyle=\rho(\theta)\Pr_{s^{\prime}\sim\sigma}(s^{\prime}_{i}(\theta_{i})=a_{i})\Pr_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i})

(from the definition of
\sigma
)

\displaystyle=\rho(\theta)\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho\\
s^{\prime}\sim\sigma\end{subarray}}(s^{\prime}_{i}(\theta_{i})=a_{i},~\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i})

\displaystyle=\rho(\theta)\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho\\
s^{\prime}\sim\sigma\end{subarray}}(s^{\prime}_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})=a_{i},~\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i})

(since
s^{\prime}_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})
can be replaced by
s^{\prime}_{i}(\theta_{i})
when
\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i}
)

\displaystyle=\rho(\theta)\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho\\
a^{\prime}\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})\end{subarray}}(a^{\prime}_{i}=a_{i},~\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i}).

(from the definition of
\sigma
)

Dividing both sides by
(\Pr_{\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i}))^{2}
, we obtain the conditional independence of
a_{i}
and
\theta_{-i}
given
\theta_{i}
:

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho\\
a^{\prime}\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})\end{subarray}}(\theta^{\prime}_{-i}=\theta_{-i},~a^{\prime}_{i}=a_{i}\mid\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i})=\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho\\
a^{\prime}\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})\end{subarray}}(\theta^{\prime}_{-i}=\theta_{-i}\mid\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i})\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{\prime}\sim\rho\\
a^{\prime}\sim\pi(\theta^{\prime})\end{subarray}}(a_{i}=a_{i}\mid\theta^{\prime}_{i}=\theta_{i}).

Next, we show the existence of an example that satisfies the conditional independence property but not the strategy representability.
We consider
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
described in
Example

2.2
again (see
Figure

3
).
Since we already proved that this
\pi
is not strategy-representable, it is sufficient to show that
\pi
satisfies the conditional independence property.
It can be verified by considering the distribution conditional on each player’s type.
If the first player’s type is
\theta_{1}
, the conditional distribution of the first player’s action and the second player’s type is the uniform distribution on
A_{1}\times\Theta_{2}
, hence conditionally independent.
This is also the case for
\theta^{\prime}_{1}
.
Since the definition of
\pi
is symmetric for two players, we can conclude that
\pi
satisfies the conditional independence property.

∎

Appendix B
Algorithm for minimizing strategy swap regret

Here we propose an algorithm for minimizing strategy swap regret
R_{\mathsf{SS},i}
for an online learning problem with stochastic types.

Since strategy swap regret
R_{\mathsf{SS},i}
is swap regret when
S_{i}
is regarded as the decision space,
we can apply the swap regret minimization algorithm proposed by
Blum and Mansour 2007
.
The direct application leads to an upper bound of
O(\sqrt{T|S_{i}|\log|S_{i}|}+|S_{i}|\log|S_{i}|)
on the strategy swap regret.

Their algorithm reduces swap regret minimization to
|S_{i}|
external regret minimization problems with decision space
S_{i}
.
In the case of strategy swap regret, we can further reduce them to
|S_{i}||\Theta_{i}|
external regret minimization problems with decision space
A_{i}
.
As a result, we obtain an upper bound of
O(\sqrt{T|S_{i}|\log|A_{i}|}+|S_{i}|\log|A_{i}|)
on the strategy swap regret, which is slightly better than the above bound.
Since
|S_{i}|=|A_{i}|^{|\Theta_{i}|}
, this bound is still exponentially large in the number of types
|\Theta_{i}|
.
Moreover, the algorithm uses exponentially many subroutines, which require exponential time computation for each round.
It is open whether there is an efficient algorithm with strategy swap regret sublinear in
T
and polynomial in
n
,
|A_{i}|
, and
|\Theta_{i}|
.
If it exists, we can compute an
\epsilon
-SFCE in polynomial time by simulating the dynamics.

Input:

The set of types
\Theta_{i}
and the set of actions
A_{i}
are specified in advance. The reward vector
u_{i}^{t}\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}
is given at the end of each round
t\in[T]
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}
be a multiplicative weights algorithm with the doubling trick
[
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 1997
, Section 4.6]
with decision space
S_{i}
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
s_{i}\in S_{i}
;

for

each round
t=1,\dots,T

do

Let
z^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}\in\Delta(A_{i})
be the output of
\mathcal{E}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}
in round
t
for each
s_{i}\in S_{i}
and
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
;

Define
P^{t}\in[0,1]^{S_{i}\times S_{i}}
by
P^{t}(s_{i},s^{\prime}_{i})=\prod_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}z^{t}_{s^{\prime}_{i},\theta_{i}}(s_{i}(\theta_{i}))
for each
s_{i},s^{\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}
;

Compute
\sigma_{i}^{t}\in\Delta(S_{i})
such that
P^{t}\sigma_{i}^{t}=\sigma_{i}^{t}
by eigenvector computation;

Let
\sigma_{i}^{t}
be the output for round
t
;

Observe reward vector
u_{i}^{t}\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}
and feed the reward vector
\tilde{u}^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}\in[0,1]^{A_{i}}
defined by
\tilde{u}^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}(a_{i})=\sigma_{i}^{t}(s_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})
for each
a_{i}\in A_{i}
to subroutine
\mathcal{E}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}
for each
s_{i}\in S_{i}
,
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
;

end for

Algorithm 4

Algorithm for minimizing strategy swap regret

The reduction proceeds as follows.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}
be the multiplicative weights algorithm with the doubling trick
[
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 1997
, Section 4.6]
with decision space
A_{i}
for each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
s_{i}\in S_{i}
.
In each round
t\in[T]
, each subroutine
\mathcal{E}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}
outputs
z^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}\in\Delta(A_{i})
.
From these outputs, we define the stochastic matrix
P^{t}\in[0,1]^{S_{i}\times S_{i}}
by
P^{t}(s_{i},s^{\prime}_{i})=\prod_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}z^{t}_{s^{\prime}_{i},\theta_{i}}(s_{i}(\theta_{i}))
for each
s_{i},s^{\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}
.
Since each
P^{t}\in\mathcal{P}
is a stochastic matrix, we can compute its stationary distribution
\sigma_{i}^{t}\in\Delta(S_{i})
that satisfies
P^{t}\sigma_{i}^{t}=\sigma_{i}^{t}
, and let
\sigma_{i}^{t}
be the decision for round
t
.
Then feed the reward vector
\tilde{u}^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}\in[0,1]^{A_{i}}
defined by
\tilde{u}^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}(a_{i})=\sigma_{i}^{t}(s_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})
for each
a_{i}\in A_{i}
to subroutine
\mathcal{E}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}
for each
s_{i}\in S_{i}
,
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
Let

R^{T}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}=\max_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{u}^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}(a_{i})-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}z_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}^{t}(a_{i})\tilde{u}^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}(a_{i})

be the external regret of
\mathcal{E}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}
for each
s_{i}\in S_{i}
and
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
Then strategy swap regret
R_{\mathsf{SS},i}^{T}
equals the sum of the external regrets as follows.

Lemma B.1
.

R_{\mathsf{SS},i}^{T}=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}R^{T}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}.

Proof.

Recall that the strategy swap regret is defined as

R_{\mathsf{SS},i}^{T}=\max_{\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{i}\sim\sigma_{i}^{t}}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i}))\right]\right]-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{i}\sim\sigma_{i}^{t}}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i}))\right]\right].

We analyze the first term and the second term separately.
The first term is

\displaystyle\max_{\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{i}\sim\sigma_{i}^{t}}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i}))\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\max_{\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}\sigma_{i}^{t}(s_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i}))

\displaystyle=\max_{\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}\tilde{u}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}^{t}((\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i}))

(from the definition of
\tilde{u}^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}\max_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{u}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}^{t}(a_{i}),

where the last equality holds because
(\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}(s_{i}))(\theta_{i})
can be optimized separately for each
s_{i}\in S_{i}
and
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.

The second term is

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta_{i}\sim\rho_{i}}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{i}\sim\sigma_{i}^{t}}\left[u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i}))\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}\sigma_{i}^{t}(s_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i},s^{\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}}P^{t}(s_{i},s^{\prime}_{i})\sigma_{i}^{t}(s^{\prime}_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i}))

(since
P^{t}\sigma_{i}^{t}=\sigma_{i}^{t}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i},s^{\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}}\sigma_{i}^{t}(s^{\prime}_{i})\prod_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}z^{t}_{s^{\prime}_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}(s_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}))u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},s_{i}(\theta_{i}))

(from the definition of
P^{t}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i},s^{\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}}\prod_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}z^{t}_{s^{\prime}_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}(s_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}))\tilde{u}_{s^{\prime}_{i},\theta_{i}}^{t}(s_{i}(\theta_{i}))

(from the definition of
\tilde{u}^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s^{\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}}\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{i}\in S_{i}\colon\\
s_{i}(\theta_{i})=a_{i}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}z^{t}_{s^{\prime}_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}}(s_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}))\right)\tilde{u}_{s^{\prime}_{i},\theta_{i}}^{t}(a_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s^{\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}}\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}z^{t}_{s^{\prime}_{i},\theta_{i}}(a_{i})\tilde{u}_{s^{\prime}_{i},\theta_{i}}^{t}(a_{i}).

Finally, we obtain

\displaystyle R_{\mathsf{SS},i}^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}\max_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{u}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}^{t}(a_{i})-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}z^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}(a_{i})\tilde{u}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}^{t}(a_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}\left\{\max_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{u}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}^{t}(a_{i})-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}z^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}(a_{i})\tilde{u}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}^{t}(a_{i})\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}R^{T}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}.\qed

By applying
Theorem

3.7
(an upper bound for the multiplicative weights with the doubling trick), we obtain an upper bound on strategy swap regret.

Theorem B.2
.

R_{\mathsf{SS},i}\leq 6\sqrt{T|S_{i}|\log|A_{i}|}+2|S_{i}|\log|A_{i}|.

Proof.

Since the total reward of each action
a_{i}\in A_{i}
for
\mathcal{E}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}
is bounded above as
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{u}^{t}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}(a_{i})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{i}^{t}(s_{i})u_{i}^{t}(\theta_{i},a_{i})\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{i}^{t}(s_{i})
, we can obtain an upper bound on the external regret

R^{T}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}\leq 6\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{i}^{t}(s_{i})\log|A_{i}|}+2\log|A_{i}|.

By summing this upper bound for all
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
s_{i}\in S_{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle R_{\mathsf{SS},i}^{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}R^{T}_{s_{i},\theta_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}\left\{6\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{i}^{t}(s_{i})\log|A_{i}|}+2\log|A_{i}|\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\left\{6\sqrt{|S_{i}|\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{i}^{t}(s_{i})\log|A_{i}|}+2|S_{i}|\log|A_{i}|\right\}

(by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\rho_{i}(\theta_{i})\left\{6\sqrt{T|S_{i}|\log|A_{i}|}+2|S_{i}|\log|A_{i}|\right\}

(since
\sum_{s_{i}\in S_{i}}\sigma_{i}^{t}(s_{i})=1
for each
t\in[T]
)

\displaystyle=6\sqrt{T|S_{i}|\log|A_{i}|}+2|S_{i}|\log|A_{i}|.\qed

Appendix C
On linear swap regret minimization

Mansour et al. 2022
defined linear swap regret for online linear optimization with a polytope constraint as follows.

Definition C.1

(Linear swap regret
[
Mansour et al. 2022
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a polytope and
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{P})
be the set of all valid linear transformations, where a linear transformation
M\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined to be valid when
Mx\in\mathcal{P}
holds for all
x\in\mathcal{P}
.
For online linear optimization with reward vector
\bar{u}^{t}\in[0,1]^{d}
for each
t\in[T]
and feasible region
\mathcal{P}
, linear swap regret is defined as

R_{\mathsf{LS}}^{T}=\max_{M\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{P})}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\langle Mx^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle-\langle x^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle\right),

where
x^{t}\in\mathcal{P}
is the algorithm’s output in each round
t\in[T]
.

Mansour et al. 2022
defined linear swap regret for a general polytope but focused on a special case of
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{X}
with applications to two-player Bayesian games.
Recall that
\mathcal{X}=\{x\in[0,1]^{\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}}\mid\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}x(\theta_{i},a_{i})=1~(\forall\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i})\}
is the set of vectors that represent each
\pi_{i}\in\Delta(A_{i})^{\Theta_{i}}
.

Here we show that linear swap regret minimization with
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{X}
can be reduced to untruthful swap regret minimization.
Note that they propose an algorithm for minimizing linear swap regret in this special case (Algorithm 2 in their paper), but focused on guarantees on the Stackelberg value and did not provide any rigorous upper bound on linear swap regret.

The following proposition claims that all valid linear transformations can be expressed by some
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, which represents a linear transformation for untruthful swap regret.
Recall that
\mathcal{Q}
is defined by (
6
).

Proposition C.2
.

For any
M\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{X})
, there exists some
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
Mx=Qx
holds for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

Fix any
M\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{X})
.
We construct
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
Mx=Qx
holds for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

Since
M
is a valid linear transformation,
Mx\in\mathcal{X}
holds for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
We use this fact for
x^{1},x^{2}\in\mathcal{X}
defined as follows.
Fix any
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a^{1}_{i},a^{2}_{i}\in A_{i}
.
Define
x^{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{1}_{i})=1
and
x(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a_{i})=0
for any other
a_{i}\in A_{i}\setminus\{a^{1}_{i}\}
.
Similarly, let
x^{2}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{2}_{i})=1
and
x^{2}(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a_{i})=0
for any other
a_{i}\in A_{i}\setminus\{a^{2}_{i}\}
.
The other entries of
x^{1}
can be defined arbitrarily, and the other entries of
x^{2}
are set to be equal to
x^{1}
.
That is, for any
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}\setminus\{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\}
, we must have
x^{1}(\theta_{i},a_{i})=x^{2}(\theta_{i},a_{i})
for any
a_{i}\in A_{i}
.

Now we use
Mx^{1}\in\mathcal{X}
and
Mx^{2}\in\mathcal{X}
.
For each
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, we have

\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}(Mx^{1})(\theta_{i},a_{i})=1\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}(Mx^{2})(\theta_{i},a_{i})=1,

which implies

\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}(M(x^{1}-x^{2}))(\theta_{i},a_{i})=0.

Since
(x^{1}-x^{2})(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{1}_{i})=1
,
(x^{1}-x^{2})(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{2}_{i})=-1
, and the other entries of
x^{1}-x^{2}
are
0
, we obtain

\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{1}_{i}))=\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{2}_{i})).

Since this holds for any pair of
a^{1}_{i},a^{2}_{i}\in A_{i}
, there exists some value
W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}
for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
such that

\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))=W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})

for any
a_{i}\in A_{i}
.
Moreover, by using
Mx\in\mathcal{X}
for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, it holds that for any
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
,

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}(Mx)(\theta_{i},a_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}x(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}x(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\left\{\sum_{a_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\left\{\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}x(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})\right\}W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})

(from the definition of
W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}).

(since
x\in\mathcal{X}
)

If every entry of
M
is included in
[0,1]
, then the proof is finished, but this does not necessarily holds.
We show that we can achieve
M\in\mathcal{Q}
by shifting each entry of
M
without changing
Mx
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
Fix any
\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
and
a_{i}\in A_{i}
, and we focus on the row
M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\cdot,\cdot))
.
For any
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, we define
a^{1}_{i}\in\argmax_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))
and
a^{2}_{i}\in\argmin_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))
.
We reuse the above definition of
x^{1}
and
x^{2}
with these new
a^{1}_{i}
and
a^{2}_{i}
.
Since
(Mx^{1})(\theta_{i},a_{i})
and
(Mx^{2})(\theta_{i},a_{i})
are included in
[0,1]
, we have

\max_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))-\min_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))\in[0,1].

(30)

If we set
x(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a_{i})=1
for some
a_{i}\in\argmax_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))
for each
\theta_{i}
and set
0
otherwise,
(Mx)(\theta_{i},a_{i})\in[0,1]
implies

\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\max_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))\in[0,1].

(31)

Similarly, we can obtain

\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\min_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))\in[0,1].

(32)

For any
\theta^{\prime}_{i},\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, even if we add an arbitrary value
C\in\mathbb{R}
to all entries
M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},\cdot))
and subtract
C
from all entries
M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},\cdot))
, the value of
(Mx)(\theta_{i},a_{i})
does not change.
If we denote this shifted matrix by
M^{\prime}
, this fact can be checked as

(M^{\prime}x)(\theta_{i},a_{i})=(Mx)(\theta_{i},a_{i})+\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}Cx(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})-\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}Cx(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})=(Mx)(\theta_{i},a_{i}).

Now we consider shifting the entries of
M
and obtain
M^{\prime}
.
Fix any
\theta^{*}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
For
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}\setminus\{\theta^{*}_{i}\}
, we define all entries
M^{\prime}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},\cdot))
by

M^{\prime}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime\prime}_{i}))=M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime\prime}_{i}))-\min_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))

for each
a^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
.
For
\theta^{*}_{i}
, we define

M^{\prime}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{*}_{i},a^{\prime\prime}_{i}))=M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{*}_{i},a^{\prime\prime}_{i}))+\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}\setminus\{\theta^{*}_{i}\}}\min_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))

for each
a^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
.
Note that the sum of shifts is
0
, and therefore,
Mx=M^{\prime}x
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
We check that all the entries of
M^{\prime}
on this row are included in
[0,1]
.
For
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}\setminus\{\theta^{*}_{i}\}
, all entries
M^{\prime}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},\cdot))
are included in
[0,1]
due to (
30
).
For
\theta^{*}_{i}
, the largest entry is

\displaystyle\max_{a^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M^{\prime}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{*}_{i},a^{\prime\prime}_{i}))

\displaystyle=\max_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{*}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))+\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}\setminus\{\theta^{*}_{i}\}}\min_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}\setminus\{\theta^{*}_{i}\}}\max_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))\in[0,1]

due to (
31
).
The smallest entry is

\min_{a^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M^{\prime}((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{*}_{i},a^{\prime\prime}_{i}))=\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\min_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}M((\theta_{i},a_{i}),(\theta^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}))\in[0,1]

due to (
32
).
Therefore, all entries of
M^{\prime}
on this row are included in
[0,1]
.

We can apply the same shifting operation to all rows of
M
and denote the obtained matrix by
Q
.
All the entries of
Q
are included in
[0,1]
with
Qx=Mx
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
From its definition,
W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})
is the sum of non-negative values, hence non-negative for each
\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
Since
\sum_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})=1
, each
W(\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{i})
is at most
1
.
Therefore,
Q
satisfies all the constraints for
\mathcal{Q}
, and we have
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
.
∎

By using this proposition, we can reduce linear swap regret minimization to untruthful swap regret minimization as

R_{\mathsf{LS}}^{T}=\max_{M\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{P})}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\langle Mx^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle-\langle x^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle\right)=\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\langle Qx^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle-\langle x^{t},\bar{u}^{t}\rangle\right),

which equals
R_{\mathsf{US},i}^{T}
from
Lemma

3.1
.

Appendix D
On Bayes coarse correlated equilibria

Here we briefly present definitions of Bayes coarse correlated equilibria for comparison with Bayes correlated equilibria.
As with SFCEs, we can define coarse correlated equilibria of the strategic form.
In an equilibrium of this class, each player
i\in N
does not have incentive to ignore recommendation
s_{i}\in S_{i}
and stick to any strategy
s^{\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}
.

Definition D.1

(Strategic-form coarse correlated equilibria (SFCCEs))
.

A distribution
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
is a strategic-form coarse correlated equilibrium
if
for any
i\in N
and any
s^{\prime}_{i}\in S_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;s^{\prime}_{i}(\theta_{i}),s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right].

Similarly, we can define coarse correlated equilibria of the agent normal form.
In an equilibrium of this class, each hypothetical player
(i,\theta_{i})\in N^{\prime}
does not have incentive to stick to any action
a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
.

Hartline et al. 2015
proposed dynamics converging to this class of equilibria.

Definition D.2

(Agent-normal-form coarse correlated equilibria (ANFCCEs))
.

A distribution
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
is an agent-normal-form coarse correlated equilibrium
if
for any
i\in N
,
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, and
a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta_{i}=\theta^{\prime}_{i}\}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;s(\theta))\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta_{i}=\theta^{\prime}_{i}\}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\sigma}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a^{\prime}_{i},s_{-i}(\theta_{-i}))\right]\right].

The incentive constraints for ANFCCEs are imposed separately for each type
\theta^{\prime}_{i}
.
For (non-coarse) ANFCEs, the separate constraints are equivalent to the total constraints (

IC
ANF

) (for the non-approximate version, i.e.,
\epsilon=0
), and the difference between SFCEs and ANFCEs comes from the difference between deviation
\phi_{\mathsf{SF}}\colon S_{i}\to S_{i}
and
\phi\colon\Theta_{i}\times A_{i}\to A_{i}
.
For SFCCEs and ANFCCEs, there is no difference in deviations, but the total constraints for SFCEs and the separate constraints for ANFCCEs are not equivalent.
If we take the summation of the incentive constraint for ANFCCEs over type
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, we obtain those for SFCCEs by setting
s^{\prime}_{i}(\theta^{\prime}_{i})=a^{\prime}_{i}
for each
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
.
On the other hand, the incentive constraints for SFCCEs do not imply those for ANFCCEs.
Thus, the set of ANFCCEs is a subset of the set of SFCCEs, which makes a striking contrast to the relation of SFCEs and ANFCEs.
A generalization of this relation for extensive-form games with imperfect information was proved by
Farina et al. 2020
.
Here we show this relation is strict even for Bayesian games by presenting a simple example.

Example D.3
.

We consider a Bayesian game with two players.
There are two possible types for each player, and their types are completely correlated.
More precisely, let
\Theta_{1}=\{\theta_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{1}\}
and
\Theta_{2}=\{\theta_{2},\theta^{\prime}_{2}\}
, and
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
realizes with probability
0.5
and
(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{2})
with probability
0.5
.
Each player has two possible actions
A_{1}=\{a_{1},a^{\prime}_{1}\}
and
A_{2}=\{a_{2},a^{\prime}_{2}\}
, respectively.
The payoffs for player
1
with each type are presented in
Table

2
.
The payoffs for player
2
are always defined to be
0
.

Table 2:
A Bayesian game for which the set of SFCCEs and the set of ANFCCEs are different. The left table is the payoffs for player
1
with type
\theta_{1}
, and the right table is the payoffs for player 1 with type
\theta^{\prime}_{1}
. The payoff for player
2
is always
0
.

a_{2}

a^{\prime}_{2}

a_{1}

0

0

a^{\prime}_{1}

0.5

0

a_{2}

a^{\prime}_{2}

a_{1}

0

1

a^{\prime}_{1}

1

0

We consider a distribution
\sigma\in\Delta(S)
that recommends two possible strategies
s,s^{\prime}
each with probability
0.5
.
For
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
, these two strategies are the same: both of
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
recommend
a_{1}
to player
1
and
a_{2}
to player
2
.
For
(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{2})
, these two strategies are different:
s_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=a^{\prime}_{1}
and
s_{2}(\theta^{\prime}_{2})=a_{2}
, while
s^{\prime}_{1}(\theta^{\prime}_{1})=a_{1}
and
s^{\prime}_{2}(\theta^{\prime}_{2})=a^{\prime}_{2}
.
The expected payoff for player
1
is
0
for
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
and
1
for
(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{2})
.
The total expected payoff is
0.5
.

First, we show that
\sigma
is an SFCCE by considering an optimal deviation for player
1
.
For
\theta_{1}
, selecting
a^{\prime}_{1}
increases payoff to
0.5
.
On the other hand, for
\theta^{\prime}_{1}
, selecting either of
a_{1}
or
a^{\prime}_{1}
decreases payoff to
0.5
.
In total, player
1
cannot increase the expected payoff.
Since the payoff for player
2
is always
0
, this player never has incentive to deviate.
Therefore,
\sigma
is an SFCCE.
On the other hand,
\sigma
is not an ANFCCE since player
1
with
\theta_{1}
can gain by choosing
a^{\prime}_{1}
.

Caragiannis et al. 2015
and
Jin and Lu 2023
used the following definition of Bayes coarse correlated equilibria.
This is similar to that of ANFCCEs, but defined on
\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
, not on
\Delta(S)
.
To our knowledge, this class has not yet been given any specific name.
A possible candidate is “coarse Bayesian solutions” since this notion is a coarse variant of Bayesian solutions.

Definition D.4

(coarse Bayesian solutions)
.

A type-wise distribution
\pi\in\Delta(A)^{\Theta}
is a coarse Bayesian solution
if
for any
i\in N
,
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\in\Theta_{i}
, and
a^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}
, it holds that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta_{i}=\theta^{\prime}_{i}\}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a)\right]\right]\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\rho}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\theta_{i}=\theta^{\prime}_{i}\}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi(\theta)}\left[v_{i}(\theta;a^{\prime}_{i},a_{-i})\right]\right].
